# PREFACE
### TO THE DISCREET
# R E A D E R.

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'I' with stylized floral motifs.</image-desc> I present to you <note>Original: "UE." an abbreviation for "U Edelen," a formal and respectful way of addressing the reader, similar to "Your Honor" or "Your Noble Self."</note> now a small Work, under the title of ALPHABETICAL LIST OF NAMES OF THE Heretics, etc., hoping that the same, because of its brevity, will be received with a favorable hand. The matters contained within it the Reader may find elsewhere in abundance; however, because not everyone is in a position to acquire for themselves so many and expensive Authors <note>In this context, "Authors" refers to the physical books written by these authorities.</note>, I have been moved to make this little Work (which I had formerly extracted and gathered from other Authors for my own pleasure and benefit) public through printing, and to share it with the discreet Reader. The principal Heretics who have appeared from the times of the Apostles until the present day, along with those who have separated themselves from the orthodox <term>orthodox: literally "right-believing," referring here to the established or mainstream church doctrines</term> Church through particular opinions, though not properly <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "on-" indicates the next page likely continues with a word such as "onder" (among) or "ontstaan" (originated).</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces an alphabetical compendium of heretics and dissenting groups, explaining that he compiled it from expensive scholarly sources to make the information accessible to general readers.</summary>
<keywords>preface, heretics, church history, religious dissent, book accessibility, orthodox church</keywords>

# PREFACE.

among the number of Heretics can be counted, but who nevertheless gained a large following, are to be found here. Those who harbored strange sentiments, but whose following was small and did not remain in existence for long, I have passed over as being of little importance. Furthermore, this little Work is enriched with an Appendix, in which the particular sects <term>Parthyen: referring here to religious factions or denominations</term>, alongside the most prominent Religious Orders in the Roman Catholic Church, are briefly brought to the Reader's attention.

Regarding the spelling and printing errors, I expect that the discreet Reader will overlook and correct them with a favorable eye; caring very little for the judgment and censures of those who are accustomed to censuring everything. Obtaining this first <note>The author is referring to his request for the reader's favorable reception and leniency regarding errors.</note>, I remain, after praying for Heaven’s most precious blessings,

->Your Noble Self's Most Obedient Servant<-
->J. A. D.<-

*Leiden, from my Study*
*on the 12th of March*
*1751.*

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' within a square frame, embellished with ornate floral scrollwork.</image-desc>ABELITES, ABELONITES, ABELIANS, a sect of Heretics, which had arisen in the countryside near *Hippo* <note>original: *Hippona*; the ancient city of Hippo Regius in modern-day Algeria, where Saint Augustine served as bishop</note>, in *Africa*, and which had already disappeared by the time of *Augustine*. They commanded that everyone should have their own [spouse], and could neither approve of nor tolerate that man should be alone; according to the institutions of the order, he had to have a helpmate <note>A reference to the biblical "help meet" for Adam in Genesis</note> who was equal to him: but it was not permitted to rely upon this helpmate; that is, to unite physically with his wife: for them, that was the Tree of Knowledge of good and evil, the fruit of which was strictly forbidden to them. ## ABRAHAMITES
, a following of heretics, whom the Arabs call *Ibrahumiah*, after their founder, who bore the name of *Ibrahim*, or *Abraham*. This heretic renewed in Antioch, where he was born, the sect of the *Paulicians* <note>A dualist Christian sect that arose in Armenia in the 7th century</note>, and had already led astray a large part of Syria; when *Cyriacus*, an orthodox Patriarch of this Church, vigorously opposed him, achieving so much that this sect was dispersed. These *Paulicians* recognized as the founder of their group *Paul of Samosata* <note>Paulus Samosetanus, a 3rd-century Bishop of Antioch who was declared a heretic for teaching that Jesus was a man who became divine, rather than God from birth</note>, who denied the divinity of Christ. The Patriarch *Cyriacus*, just mentioned, held the see at Antioch under the reign of *Harun al-Rashid*, Caliph of the *Abbasid* dynasty, around 805 years after Christ's birth. *Nicephorus* was then Emperor in the East, and *Charlemagne* <note>Charles the Great</note> in the West. *Herbelot, Oriental Library.* <note>Referencing the 'Bibliothèque Orientale' by Barthélemy d'Herbelot, a famous 17th-century encyclopedia of Middle Eastern history</note>

## ABSTINENTS or ABSTAINERS 
<term>Abstinenten: from the Latin 'abstinere', meaning to refrain or hold back</term> were certain heretics at the end of the 3rd century who arose in France and Spain, at the same time when the Church was oppressed by the persecutions of the Emperors *Diocletian* and *Maximian*. They were from the *Gnosti-* <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "ti-" suggests it continues as "Gnostici" or Gnostics</note>

# Alphabetical List of Names AC

[Gnos]tics and *Manicheans*; they rejected marriage; condemned the eating of meat as something invented by the Devil, and placed the *Holy Spirit* among the ranks of created beings. *Philastrius Chapter 26. Baronius, the year of Christ 288. Prateolus under the word Abstinents.* <note>Philastrius was a 4th-century bishop; Baronius was a 16th-century Cardinal known for his ecclesiastical history; Prateolus (Gabriel du Préau) was a 16th-century theologian who wrote a catalog of heretics.</note>

**ACEPHALII or ACEPHALANS**, a certain type of Heretics who were named thus because they had no head <note>From the Greek *akephalos*, meaning "headless"</note> over them. Some writers believe they are those who wished to follow neither Patriarch *John* of *Antioch* nor *Cyril* of *Alexandria* in the dispute that existed during the time of *Sixtus III*, after the assembly of the Council of *Ephesus*. However, it is more likely that they arose around the year 482, and that this name was given to those who followed the errors of *Peter Mongus* <note>Also known as "the Stammerer," a 5th-century Patriarch of Alexandria</note>, Bishop of *Alexandria*. The *Acephalans* abandoned him because he appeared to approve the decrees of the Council of *Chalcedon* <note>The Council of 451 AD which defined the dual nature of Christ—truly God and truly man—against the Monophysite view</note>, which they held in abhorrence. Some others say that these Heretics followed the errors of *Severus*, Bishop of *Antioch*, who nevertheless came after them and held a particular view. They refused to tolerate any authority or government, and, alongside *Eutyches* <note>A monk whose teachings led to the Eutychian heresy, claiming Christ’s humanity was absorbed by his divinity</note>, they conflated the two natures of *Christ*, and for this reason were condemned by the Council of *Chalcedon* in the 5th century. *Liberatus in his brief, Chapter 9. Leontius on the Sects, Act 5. Baronius.*

<header>AD of the Heretics.</header>

*Epiphanius* <note>A 4th-century bishop of Salamis and author of the *Panarion*, a famous treatise against heresies</note> relates that this name was given to the *Manichean* Heretics of *Mesopotamia*, on account of a certain *Acovana*, a disciple of *Manes* <note>The founder of Manichaeism, a religion that taught a dualistic struggle between light and darkness</note>, who spread his errors in that land.

## ADAMITES

*ADAMITES*, *Epiphanius*, *Augustine*, and subsequently *Theodoret* make mention of a sect of infamous Heretics whom they call *Adamites*. It is believed that this sect was an offshoot of the *Basilidians* and the *Carpocratians* <note>Followers of Basilides and Carpocrates, 2nd-century Gnostic teachers in Alexandria</note>. For *Irenaeus*, in the 1st Chapter of his 1st Book, *Against Heresies* <note>original: "Adversus Haereses"</note> says,

> “that some, based on the principles of the teachings of *Basilides* and of *Carpocrates*, permitted an infamous social intercourse with all sorts of persons, the multiplication of marriages, and the freedom to eat meat that had been offered in sacrifice to the gods of the heathens.” 

But these errors are not exactly the same as those attributed to the *Adamites*. *Theodoret* identifies *Prodicus* as the founder of the *Adamites*. Mention is made of this *Prodicus* by *Tertullian* and by *Clement of Alexandria*; and the latter accuses his disciples of giving themselves every freedom to commit adultery in secret, and to give themselves over to all kinds of lusts. He also notes that they judged prayer to be unnecessary. *Clement of Alexandria* in his 3rd and 7th Books. *Tertullian* places him, alongside *Valentinus* <note>A prominent Gnostic theologian</note>, in the number of Heretics who denied the unity of a single God, and rejected the necessity of the suffering <note>referring to the Passion or physical suffering of Christ</note>. *Tertullian, Against Praxeas, chapter 3.* <note>original: "Tertull. contra Praxem. c. 3."</note>

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Prodicus and the origins of the Adamite sect, transitioning from references in Tertullian and Clement of Alexandria to the descriptions provided by Epiphanius and Augustine.</meta>

<header>Alphabetical Name List AD</header>

in *Scorpiace* <note>original: "Scorpiaco"; a work by the early Christian author Tertullian intended to provide an antidote against the "sting" of Gnostic heresies</note> chapter 15. *Clement* of *Alexandria* adds to this that the disciples of *Prodicus* boasted of having secret books of *Zoroaster* <note>An ancient Persian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism; Gnostic groups often claimed his authority</note>, in Book 1 of his *Stromata* <note>Clement's "Miscellanies," a major work of early Christian philosophy</note>. But none of the aforementioned writers give the disciples of *Prodicus* the name of *Adamites*. *Epiphanius* is the first who mentions them, without saying that they were disciples of *Prodicus*. He placed them between the *Alogians* and *Sampseans*, after the *Montanists*, and before the *Theodotians*, roughly around the end of the second century. The impieties that he attributes to them are that they held their meetings in a heated room <note>original: "Kagchel"; likely referring to a hypocaust or a bathhouse-style heated room</note>, and that they, both men and women, entered it naked; that they sat there among one another, and in that state read and performed their prayers. They nevertheless boasted of the gift of abstinence <note>original: "onthouding"; the practice of self-restraint, particularly in regard to sexual relations</note>, and asserted that if anyone fell into sin, they would cast him out of their assembly, just as *Adam* was driven out of the earthly *Paradise* because he had eaten of the forbidden fruit; they regarded themselves as *Adam* and *Eve*, and their temple as *Paradise*. For which reason they are also named *Adamites*. See there all that *Epiphanius* says of their impieties, in the 52nd Heresy. *Augustine* adds that they had a horror of marriage, because *Adam* had not "known" <note>A biblical euphemism for sexual relations</note> his wife until after he had sinned and departed from *Paradise*. *Theodoret*, in the 1st Book of the *Compendium of Heretical Fables* <note>original: "fabelen der Ketteryen," referring to his "Haereticarum Fabularum Compendium"</note>, makes *Prodicus* the founder of this sect, as we have said, and attributes to them that they allowed women [to be held] in co-

### Alphabetical Name List AD to Æ

and who had as heads of their Sect the Bishops *Felix* <note>Felix, Bishop of Urgell (died 818 AD), a leading proponent of Adoptianism</note> and *Elipand* <note>Elipandus, Archbishop of Toledo (c. 717–808 AD)</note>. They taught that *Jesus Christ*, with respect to his Divine Nature, was the true and proper Son of God, but that with respect to his human nature, he was nothing other than [the Son] through adoption (*Adoptio*) <note>The theological concept that Jesus was the Son of God by grace or "adoption" in his humanity, rather than being the natural Son of God in both natures</note> and grace. This Heresy was also called the *Felician*, and was an offshoot of the Heresy of the *Nestorians* <note>A 5th-century movement that emphasized the separation of the divine and human natures of Christ</note>, since it divided *Jesus Christ* as if into two Sons, and as if into two Persons. It was condemned in the Council of Frankfurt, which *Charlemagne* <note>Karel de Groot; King of the Franks and the first Holy Roman Emperor</note> assembled in the year 794. *Hornius, Church History.*

**ADRIANISTS**, thus certain Heretics are named by *Theodoretus* <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), an influential theologian and church historian</note>, who places them among the number of Heretics who sprang from the Sect of *Simon Magus* (a) <note>original: "Simon den Tovenaar." Simon Magus is a biblical figure considered by many early church fathers to be the first heretic</note>, but no other Writer is found who speaks of these Heretics. *Theodoret, Book 1, Heretical Fables, Chapter 1.* In the 16th century, there was also a similar Heresy in England, which followed one *Adrian Hamstet* <note>Adriaan Hamstede (c. 1525–1563), a Dutch preacher in London whose tolerance for Anabaptists led to his excommunication</note>, and contained very offensive and Anabaptist sentiments.

**ÆSCHINES**, a Heretic, being devoted to the errors of *Montanus* <note>Founder of the Montanist movement, which focused on new prophetic revelations and strict asceticism</note>, whose followers distinguished themselves from other *Montanists* in that they taught that *Christ* was both the Father and the Son in the *Holy Trinity*. *Epiphanius & Philostorgius, On Heresies; appendix to Tertullian, On the Prescription of Heretics, Chapter 52.*

->AE of the Heretics.<-
A 5

### ETHNOPHRONES, ETHNOPHRONEN, or PAGANISANS,
were Heretics in the 7th century, who indeed presented themselves as Christians, but followed the customs of the Pagans, and practiced fortune-telling, sorcery, and other follies. *John of Damascus <note>original: "Joh. Damasc." A Syrian monk and influential theologian (c. 675–749 AD)</note>; Sanders, Heresies 226; Gualtieri in Chronology.*

### AETIANS
were Heretics and followers of *Arius* <note>The priest from Alexandria whose teachings on the nature of Christ led to the Arian controversy</note> and *Aëtius*, called the *Atheist*, or *Denier of God*. They made up the faction that was called the pure *Arians*, and their error was embraced by *Eunomius*, the principal disciple of *Aëtius*; by *Eudoxius*; by *Acacius of Caesarea*; by *George of Alexandria*, and by most *Western Arians*. They were also called *Eunomians*, or *Anomeans*, after the disciple *Eunomius*. And because they taught that the Son not only had a different Essence than the Father, but was unlike him in everything <note>original Greek: "κατὰ παντα ἀνόμοιον" (kata panta anomoion)</note>, they were also named *Anomii*, *Anomæi*, and *Anomiani* <note>These terms all derive from the Greek word for "unlike" or "dissimilar"</note>. *Theodosius the Great* † drove them out of all his cities; for which reason they stayed here and there in the wildernesses, and came together in secret in private houses, for which reason they were also called *Troglodytes* <note>From the Greek for "cave-dwellers," a name given to them because they were forced to meet in hidden or subterranean places</note>. *Sozomen, Book 7, Chapter 26; Theodoret, Book 4, Heretical Fables.*

a faction of Heretics, who at the end of the 7th century,

They forbade the consumption of *Meat*, and rejected *Marriage*, as if it had not been instituted by God. **AGNOITANS**, or **AGNOETES**, a sect of Heretics in the 4th century, who followed the errors of *Theophronius of Cappadocia*, who claimed that the knowledge by which God foresees future things, knows the present, and remembers the past, is not the same; which he attempted to prove through some passages from the Holy Scripture. The *Eunomians* <note>An extreme Arian sect that believed the Son was of a different nature than the Father</note>, being unable to tolerate this Teaching, expelled him from their community, and he established himself as the head of a Sect, to which the name *Eunomiophronians* was also given. They also changed the *Baptism*, no longer baptizing in the name of the Holy Trinity, but in the name of the death of *Jesus Christ*. This *Theophronius* had written a Book on the exercise of the spirit, in which he asserted his new opinions, for which he was driven out by the *Eunomians*. It was around the year 370, under the reign of *Valens* <note>Eastern Roman Emperor from 364 to 378 AD</note>, when *Theophronius* became the head of a particular sect. *Socrates, Book 5, Chapter 24; Sozomen, Book 6, Chapter 26, & Book 7, Chapter 27; Nicephorus, Book 12, Chapter 30; Prateolus under the word Agnoites.*

### ALBIGENSES,
commonly called ALBIGOTTES in their time. It is the unanimous opinion of both Roman Catholic and Protestant writers that the ALBIGENSES were a group of people who, in the 12th and the two following centuries, heavily opposed the Roman Church in both doctrine and ceremonies, and especially regarding the supremacy of the Pope and the authority of their Church; and that they were therefore also condemned by that Church as heretics, and persecuted with fire and sword <note>A reference to the Albigensian Crusade and the Inquisition</note>. They were called ALBIGENSES after the very ancient city of Albi, located in France, in the province of Languedoc on the river Tarn, where they first revealed themselves and held their primary residence, and from where they then spread through all of Provence, Languedoc, Bearn, and other surrounding lands—indeed, even subsequently across all of Europe. But as to what their specific beliefs were, besides those in which they opposed the Roman Church, there is no agreement.

Several writers of the Roman Church, and among them primarily *Bossuet* <note>Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet (1627–1704), a prominent French bishop and theologian</note>, first Bishop of Condom and thereafter of Meaux, are of the opinion that the ALBIGENSES held the beliefs of the *Manichaeans* <note>A dualistic religion that saw the world as a struggle between good/spirit and evil/matter</note>, brought over to them from Bulgaria, and those of the *Arians* <note>Those who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, and also that their way of life...

<meta>catchword: whole</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Albigenses, a prominent medieval group labeled as heretics for opposing the Roman Catholic Church's authority and ceremonies, specifically noting their origins in Albi, France.</summary>
<keywords>Albigenses, Albigottes, Albi, Languedoc, Pope, Heresy, Persecution, Bossuet, Manichaeans, Arians, France</keywords>

The learned *Limborch* <note>Philipp van Limborch (1633–1712), a Dutch Remonstrant theologian famous for his history of the Inquisition</note>, a Protestant, seems to be of the same opinion in his *History of the Inquisition*, maintaining that the actual *Albigenses* were *Manichaeans*, and must therefore be carefully distinguished from the *Waldensians*. He builds his opinion primarily on the fact that in the *Sentences* <note>The formal judicial pronouncements or "Vonnissen"</note> of the Inquisition of Toulouse, which he published from the original, many were accused of the views of the *Manichaeans*, and having been convicted of them, were condemned.

However, he is learnedly and kindly refuted by *Basnage* <note>Jacques Basnage (1653–1723), a prominent French Protestant historian</note> in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 24, chapter 5, pages 1410 and 1411. But *Bossuet* <note>Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet, the Catholic bishop mentioned on the previous page</note> grounds himself on the testimony of two monks who fought against the *Albigenses* regarding matters of religion: namely, *Alanus* <note>Alain de Lille (c. 1128–1202), a Cistercian monk and theologian</note> of the *Cistercian* order, and *Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay* <note>A chronicler who accompanied the Albigensian Crusade</note> of the same order. These authors accuse the *Albigenses* of the following, concerning their doctrine:

1. That they recognized two principles from which all things were brought forth, or two *Creators*: a good one, *Creator* of invisible and spiritual things, and an evil one, *Creator* of bodies and protector of the Old Testament.

2. That they posited two *Christs*: one evil, who came into the world; and another good, who nevertheless never came into the world.

Inquisitor who had good knowledge, gives this testimony in his Chronicle concerning them: "There were at that time various types of Heretics; some were *Arians* <note>Followers of Arius who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>; others *Manichaeans* <note>A dualistic sect that believed in a struggle between a good spiritual world and an evil material world</note>; the third were *Vaudois* <note>Waldensians, a movement focused on poverty and lay preaching that predated the Reformation</note>. These all joined together to oppose the Catholic faith, but in the meantime they did not cease to set themselves against one another: and the latter [the Vaudois] disputed with great shrewdness against the *Manichaeans*. These disputes of the *Vaudois* made the Priests ashamed and ignited their hatred. They [the priests] had more indulgence for the *Manichaeans* than for them. At that time it was a great shame to be a Priest, so much so that they commonly covered their tonsures <note>original: "kroonen," literally 'crowns,' the circular shaved patch on a priest’s head that identified their office</note>. The *Bishops* performed their office as best they could, and their authority was so despised that even the *Nobility* (or *Soldiers*) publicly adhered to this heresy: the resistance of the *Clergy* was in vain, and there was a deep respect for these *Sectarians*. They were allowed cemeteries to bury their dead; they received more gifts than the Clergy; they were free from the usual taxes, and from military expeditions <note>original: "tocht"</note> and watch duty; and if anyone traveled with them, his enemy never dared to do him harm. *In the Chronicle, page 49.*" *Bernard Gui* <note>The text uses the phonetic spelling 'Beruard Geudon'; Gui was a famous Dominican Inquisitor and author of the 'Manual for Inquisitors'</note>, of the order of *Dominicans*, or Preachers, who had long been an Inquisitor in *Toulouse* against the *Albigenses*, and therefore knew them well, writes much the same in his Chronicle of *Toulouse*. ## Alphabetical Name List AL 18

[The Count of] Toulouse, supporter of the *Albigenses* <note>A group of medieval Christians in Southern France who sought a simpler, more apostolic life, but were declared heretics by the Catholic Church</note>, has been accused of being a *Manichaean* <note>original: "Manicheen"; a follower of a dualistic religion that believes in a struggle between a good spiritual world and an evil material world. In the Middle Ages, this was a common slur for any group rejecting church authority</note>. However, *Bertrand*, a famous jurist of Toulouse who lived about 200 years ago, says that he has seen an old handwritten *history* in which that Count is declared entirely free of that crime. He suggests the Count was only accused by the [neighboring] Princes because they envied his virtue and because they sought to divide his goods among themselves.

3. From the confessions of the *Albigenses* themselves, some of which are even recorded by their enemies, and others discovered in old writings. One may fairly judge a religious group by the views held by its leaders. Considering these views of the leaders of the *Albigenses*, one finds that *Peter de Bruys* <note>Pierre de Bruys (died c. 1131), a popular preacher whose followers were known as Petrobrusians</note> was never accused of the views of the *Manichaeans*. Instead, he was only charged with five specific matters, namely: "that he rejected *infant baptism* <note>original: "Kinderendoop"</note>, because children do not believe, nor can they be taught; and he believed that one had no need for *altars* or *churches*; that one must not worship the *Cross* as the instrument of Christ's suffering; that the *Mass* was nothing, and that the body and blood of Christ were not *consecrated* <note>original: "Geconſacreert"; the ritual act of transforming the bread and wine</note> therein; and that one must not pray for the dead." Thus states the Abbot *Fleury*, later *Bishop of Fréjus*, and Teacher...

[concerning whether] the body of *Christ* was essentially or actually in the *Host* <note>The bread used in the Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> through the *Consecration* <note>The ritual act of sanctifying the bread and wine</note>. For which reason he was also scourged, his goods declared forfeit, and banished. From *Durandus of Huesca* <note>original: "Duraud van Ofca"; a Spanish Waldensian who later reconciled with the Catholic Church and wrote about his former beliefs</note>, another teacher of the *Albigenses*, we have a confession of faith which in its main points agrees with the doctrine of the *Reformed Church*, and is directly opposed to those of the *Manichaeans* <note>A dualistic heresy that believed in two opposing gods of good and evil; the Albigenses were frequently—and, as this author argues, falsely—accused of this</note>. And as the leaders felt, so too did the members, as appears from the confession of faith that the *Albigenses* made in the *Council of Lombers* <note>A meeting held in 1165 or 1176 in France where the Albigenses were publicly examined</note>, held in the year 1176, in which all the Bishops of the neighboring places were present. > „ That there is one

These are no *Manichaeans*. It appears no less clearly that they were not *Manichaeans* from an ancient manuscript containing several judicial proceedings of the *Inquisition* against the *Albigenses*, owned by Mr. *Graverol* <note>François Graverol (1635–1694), a prominent Huguenot lawyer and historian from Nîmes</note> at *Nîmes*. quite the opposite. As a specimen of this, one may look at the *Interrogatories* <note>A formal set of questions used in a legal or ecclesiastical examination</note> put to *Willem van Maunbeac*, along with his answers given in the presence of five *Monk Inquisitors* and a *Notary*, who drew up the *Record*. The accused refused to take the oath demanded of him, because the *Albigenses* <note>A medieval religious group in Southern France often targeted by the Inquisition</note> did not swear oaths. He was asked whether *Pope Martin*, who sat upon the *Roman See*, had the power to bind or to loose? <note>Referring to the biblical "Power of the Keys" (Matthew 16:19), the authority the Catholic Church claims to forgive sins or excommunicate</note> He answered,

> I recognize no other Head of the faith than *Jesus Christ*; no man on earth having the power to arrogate that right to himself, nor does any Church have the right to play the master over others.

Furthermore, he was asked whether the *Prelates* <note>High-ranking clergy such as bishops</note> of the *Roman Church* had the power to bind or to loose? He answered no. He was asked whether *Baptism*, administered by a *Priest* who says, "I baptize you," etc., effects the forgiveness of sins? He answered,

> no carnal man can administer *Baptism* with the forgiveness of sins; that depends on God alone.

He further rejected the last *Anointing* <note>Extreme Unction, the ritual anointing of the dying</note> and *confirmation*, and would recognize neither as *Sacraments* of the Church, any more than *Holy Orders* <note>The ordination of priests</note> and *confession*. He believed that in the *Lord’s Supper*, after the *Consecration* <note>The prayer used to bless the bread and wine</note>, the *Bread* remained *Bread*, and that one does God an injustice by saying that the *Bread* is changed into the body of *Christ*. To this, add the confession of faith of the *Albigenses* found in *Léger* in his *History of the Waldensians*, page 26, which is old and authentic. The treatise on the *Antichrist*

written in the year 1120, and thus before *Waldus* <note>Peter Waldo (c. 1140 – c. 1205), the leader of the Waldensians</note>, from whom the aforementioned *Bossuet* <note>Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet (1627–1704), a prominent French Catholic bishop and historian</note> claims the *Vaudois* (*Waldensians*) received their name, was even in the world, also gives a vivid description of the *Albigenses’* faith, which corresponds with that of the *Reformed Church*.

From the testimonies of those who had dealings with the *Albigenses*, or heard something of them, and who are commonly cited to prove that they were *Manichaeans* <note>Followers of a dualistic religion that the medieval Church used as a catch-all label for heretics</note>. *St. Bernard* <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a major church leader and mystic</note> among others, who was in *Toulouse* to convert those alleged Heretics, acknowledges—even if, as he himself admits, in vain—that there were various types of Heretics around Albi and Toulouse who differed in opinion, although they were all against the *Roman Church*; and those with whom he had the most to do, he nowhere accuses of the opinions of the *Manichaeans*. His dispute with them is primarily over the Sacraments, and one must conclude that he acquits them of those terrible opinions when he says,

> "All Heresies have a Father to whom they owe their origin. The *Nestorians* <note>Followers of Nestorius who held specific views on the nature of Christ</note> have *Nestorius*; the *Arians* <note>Followers of Arius who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, *Arius*; the *Manichaeans*, *Manes* <note>The founder of Manichaeism</note>; but this *Sect* can point to neither its origin nor its Father."

It is true that in his Sermons on the Song of Solomon <note>original: *Hooglied van Salomon*</note> he appears to oppose the opinions of the *Manichaeans*, but nowhere is it found that he says the *Albigenses* share in them. See also the letter of *Everwyn* <note>Evervinus of Steinfeld, who wrote to St. Bernard about heretics in the Rhineland</note>, Provost of Steinfeld in Westphalia,

### AL of the Heretics.

[Evervinus of Steinfel]len, of the order of the *Premonstratensians* <note>A Roman Catholic religious order of canons regular founded in 1120</note>, under the *Archbishopric* of *Cologne*, written to *St. Bernard* and to be found in the *Analecta* <note>A collection of shorter literary or historical pieces</note> of *Mabillon* <note>Jean Mabillon (1632–1707), a French Benedictine monk and scholar considered the founder of paleography</note>, 3rd volume, page 456, in which he reports that there were two distinct *Religions* <note>meaning two distinct sectarian groups</note>, enemies of one another, which he describes in such a way that one can see there certainly were those who held the fundamental principles of the *Manichaeans* <note>A dualistic religion that believed in a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness</note>; but also others who, except for the matter of infant baptism and the swearing of oaths, were for the rest in agreement with the sentiments of the *Reformed Church*: just as the learned *Mabillon*, in his notes on his aforementioned *Analecta*, expressly says that one must distinguish the views of *Peter de Bruys* <note>A 12th-century French reformer who rejected infant baptism and the veneration of the cross</note> from those of the *Manichaeans*; see this at length by the *Abbot Fleury* in his *Ecclesiastical History*, 14th volume, page 648, etc. *Peter*, *Abbot of Cluny* <note>Peter the Venerable (c. 1092–1156), who was a contemporary and opponent of the Petrobrusians</note>, who also lived at that time, far from giving bad testimony of the *Albigenses*, also shows that they were free from the sentiments of the *Manichaeans*, because he only refutes the five *Articles* of *Peter* and *Henry de Bruys* <note>Henry of Lausanne, another contemporary reformer and follower of Peter de Bruys</note>. See the aforementioned *Ecclesiastical History* of the *Abbot Fleury*, 14th volume, page 637, etc. Even at the *Council of Oxford* in *England*, held in the year 1160, the *Albigenses* are indeed noted as old Heretics, spread through many lands, yet having nothing in common with the *Manichaeans*. So it is sufficient to conclude how little credit the aforementioned *Alanus* <note>Alain de Lille (c. 1128–1202), a French theologian</note> and *Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay* <note>A Cistercian monk and chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade, known for his intense bias against the heretics</note> deserve, having committed such clumsy errors in the *History* of their time, and being prejudiced with a great passion against the *Albigenses*.

Remarkable is the testimony of *Du Tillet* <note>Jean du Tillet (d. 1570), a French bishop and historian who studied original state documents</note> in his *Book of the Wars against the Albigenses*. This writer, Clerk of the Parliament of Paris, and who testifies that he composed his history from the **King's Archives**, clearly acquits them of the evil laid to their charge, and judges that they were of the same opinion as the *Reformed* <note>The "Gereformeerden," referring to the Protestant tradition following Calvin</note>, which is why war was justly <note>In the view of the Catholic authorities of the time</note> waged against them. *De Serres* <note>Jean de Serres (1540–1598), a French Protestant advisor to Henry IV and a historian</note> in his *History of France* on *Louis the Eighth*, page 26.

It is well known that *Peter and Henry de Bruys*, alongside *Arnold* <note>Arnold of Brescia, a 12th-century reformer</note>, were the leaders of the *Albigenses*, and it is manifest from what has been said what their initial sentiments were; this can be inferred from what was previously cited from Lords *Fleury* <note>Claude Fleury (1640–1723), a French church historian</note> and *De Maria* <note>As noted on the previous page, this refers to Pierre de Marca (1594–1662), Archbishop of Toulouse</note>. ### ...of the Heretics.

...the, one can conclude from what has already been reported, that they were pure and godly in their conduct, through which their esteem and following increased, while that of the Priests fell; this is sufficiently evident from the cited testimony of *Guill. de Puy Laurent* <note>Guillaume de Puylaurens (c. 1200–1275), a chronicler who wrote about the Albigensian Crusade</note>.

They have various names among the ancient Writers; after *Pieter de Brueis* <note>Peter of Bruys</note>, they were called *Pedro-Brusianen* <note>Petrobrusians</note>; after *Hendrik de Brueis* <note>Henry of Lausanne, a reformist monk</note>, *Henricianen* <note>Henricians</note>; after *Arnold* <note>Arnold of Brescia</note>, *Arnoldisten* <note>Arnoldists</note>; and because of the purity of their behavior and way of life, they were called *Catharen* <note>Cathars, from the Greek word for "pure"</note>, *Puritains* <note>Puritans</note>, and *Goede Lieden* <note>Good Folk or "Bons Hommes"</note>. They were also sometimes called *Pataryns*, or *Patarinen* <note>Patarines</note>, which according to *Sigonius* <note>Carlo Sigonio (1524–1584), an Italian scholar</note> originated from a small place in the Milanese territory called *Pataro*, where they first revealed themselves. They were also called *Vaudoizen* <note>Waldenses</note>, with whom they formed a community: indeed, one must conclude from both ancient and later Writers that they were one and the same; although perhaps the name *Vaudoizon* was of a more general extent than that of *Albigensen* <note>Albigenses</note>, it is at least certain that they lived in the same places in *Provence* and *Languedoc*, taught the same doctrine, and also had to endure and undergo the same slanders and persecutions: and the learned Jesuit *Gretser* <note>Jacob Gretser (1562–1625)</note> may rightly say that the *Inquisiteurs* <note>Inquisitors</note> often confused the *Albigensen* and *Vaudoizen* with one another, just as *Heer de Maria* <note>Pierre de Marca (1594–1662), a French historian and bishop</note> and others have also done, as can be seen from the aforementioned account. Against this, it is argued that the name of the *Vaudoizen* is not as old as that of the *Albigensen*, because it is believed that the former only began—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins and spread of the Waldensians and Albigensians, following a list of the various names they were called by in different regions.</meta>

# Alphabetical List of Names: AL

...[origi]nated in the latter part of the 12th century, when *Peter Waldo* <note>Petrus Waldus (c. 1140 – c. 1205), a wealthy merchant from Lyon who gave away his property and began preaching a life of poverty</note> rose up in *Lyon*; but this is not so certain. *Pilichdorf* <note>Peter of Pilichdorf, a 14th-century writer known for his treatises against the Waldensians</note>, a writer of that time, writes that the *Waldensians* are named after a city in *France* called *Walden*, and revealed themselves in the year 1130, and thus long before *Waldo*. It is also very probable that they received their name earlier from *Valdo*, that great friend of *Berengarius* <note>Berengar of Tours (c. 999–1088), a theologian who famously disputed the doctrine of transubstantiation</note>.

In a short time, they filled not only *Provence* and *Languedoc*, but also all the surrounding Provinces, and even penetrated into *Spain*, primarily into *Aragon*. No sooner had they revealed themselves than they were persecuted. The Abbot of *Castres* began the work, but he was halted by the secular authorities. In the 12th century, *Peter of Bruys* <note>Pieter de Brueis, a popular reformer who was eventually lynched by a mob for burning crosses</note> rose up, having previously been a *Roman Priest*, but after a ministry of 20 years, he was burned in the year 1126. The *Albigensians* began to cause the Pope to fear; he sent a Legate <note>a personal representative of the Pope</note> to *France*, and specifically to *Toulouse*, to restrain those heretics, and he also had *Henry of Bruys* <note>Hendrik de Brueis, also known as Henry of Lausanne, a prominent critic of the corruption in the Church</note> secretly burned. But just as it has been in all times, so it was there as well: "the blood of the martyrs was the seed of the Church" <note>This refers to the famous phrase by the early Christian author Tertullian: *Sanguis martyrum semen christianorum*</note>. For through the perseverance of these martyrs, the people began to be moved: and neither the *sermons of St. Bernard* <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), one of the most influential monks and preachers of the Middle Ages</note>, nor his alleged *miracles*—such as using a blessed *Host* <note>the bread or wafer used in the Eucharist</note> to supposedly cure all kinds of diseases—nor all the curses of the Pope and the Councils, nor secret violence, could halt their progress.

<summary>This page discusses the historical debate surrounding the origins of the Waldensians, their rapid spread across Southern France and Spain, and the early martyrs like Peter of Bruys whose executions only served to increase the movement's popularity.</summary>
<keywords>Waldensians, Peter Waldo, Pilichdorf, Peter of Bruys, Albigensians, Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, martyrdom, religious persecution, Lyon, Toulouse, Provence</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the rise of the Albigensian movement in Southern France and the early attempts by the Catholic Church to suppress them through trials, military action, and formal condemnation at councils.</meta>

->### Alphabetical List of Heretics. 29<-

The cities of *Toulouse*, *Albi*, *Carcassonne*, *Béziers*, *Agde*, *Castres*, *Lavaur*, and all the countryside and cities of *Languedoc* <note>a historical region in southern France where the Albigensian movement was most prominent</note> adopted the teachings of the *Albigensians*, which caused the persecutions to increase. There, use was made of the water ordeal <note>original: *waterproef*; an ancient judicial practice where the accused was thrown into water; it was believed that the pure element of water would reject the guilty, causing them to float</note>; also used to test witches, it was employed there to investigate who were heretics. Those who floated on the water were heretics and had to be put to death, but those who sank were free <note>proving their innocence through the "acceptance" of the water</note>. In the year 1176, the Legate <note>a high-ranking diplomat representing the Pope</note> of the Pope took to the field with a large army. He besieged *Lavaur* and captured it. In this same year, the *Bishops* from around *Toulouse* gathered at *Lombers*, where the *Albigensians* did appear, though they would not recognize that assembly as their judge. Instead, they made the previously noted confession before the people and were condemned. In the following year, the Legate came to *Toulouse*, where he sought and found various heretics whom he excommunicated and drove out of the city.

In the year 1179, Pope *Alexander* <note>Pope Alexander III, who reigned from 1159 to 1181</note>—who had already previously caused the *Albigensians* to be condemned at the Council of *Tours* in France in his presence—convened a Council in the Church of the Lateran in *Rome*. There, he once again condemned them—not because they were *Manichaeans* <note>followers of a dualistic belief system that the Church used as a label for heretics to justify harsher punishments</note>, but as people who made a public confession of their faith and remained in *Albi*—along with the *Patarines* <note>a name given to various groups that opposed the wealth and corruption of the clergy</note>, who are *Waldensians*, and he pronounced a curse upon them. However, it was not yet established at that time that violence should be used against them. To that end, all the various sects united, not in fee-

...opinions <note>original: *gevoelens*; this continues from the previous page's discussion of the Albigensian beliefs or "feelings"</note>, but rather to repel force with force, since they foresaw that further violence would be used against them. They had as their champion the Count of *Toulouse*, along with other neighboring *Princes* and *Nobles*, whom the authorities sought to deprive of their property because of their faith.

*Innocent III*, of the *House* of *Conti*—reckoned from that time as a learned man whose personal conduct was largely beyond reproach, yet who sought to promote the *Papal Authority* with all his might—sent the *Cardinal* of *St. Mary* as a *Legate* <note>a high-ranking diplomatic representative of the Pope</note> to *France* in the year 1198. The reasons for this mission were a matter of dispute. Shortly thereafter, the *Council* of *Sens* convened; however, that *Legate* was not present, much less did he act as *President*. There, a *Sentence* was passed against the *Abbot* of *St. Martin* and the *Dean* of the *Cathedral* of *Nevers*; because they were *Albigensians*, the former was removed from his office and the latter was suspended from his. This Council stated that the *Sect* of the *Albigensians* had taken deep root in the Realm and possessed powerful *Champions* <term>Voorstanders</term> <note>original: *Voorftanders*; influential protectors among the local nobility who shielded the movement from the Church</note>.

Around this time, they began to use more overt public violence against the *Albigensians*, and a *crusade* was launched against them to root them out by fire and sword. The Pope sent three Legates to *France*: *Arnauld*, Abbot General of the *Cistercians* <note>a Roman Catholic religious order of enclosed monks</note>; *Raoul*, a monk of the same order; and *Peter* of *Chateauneuf*, otherwise called *Castelnau*. It was claimed that they met with great success, as the *Albigensians* either fled or were converted. In the year 1205, a meeting of the parties was...

In the year 1206, the Legates arrived in *Toulouse*, where they found the Bishops in a sorry state because the *Monks* were swallowing up the tithes <note>a mandatory tax of one-tenth of one's income paid to the church</note>, and the *Albigensians* refused to give anything to the Clergy, which made them even more hated. *Dominic* and others began to preach against these irregularities. Various meetings were also held, of which the one at *Montreal* near *Carcassonne* was the most significant. Regarding these meetings, some of the Roman Catholic writers say that the mouths of the *Albigensians* were stopped, so that they could no longer speak. Yet one doubts this with good reason, as the monk *Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay* <note>original: *de Val Cernay*; a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note> relates that the Judges, who were laymen, dared not pass judgment because they would then have condemned themselves, since they were *Albigensians*. But how is this to be reconciled? One must therefore conclude that it did not end very much to the liking and satisfaction of the Romans. *Raymond*, Count of *Toulouse*, was at the head of the *Albigensians* <note>a medieval religious sect in Southern France deemed heretical by the Catholic Church</note>. In the year 1207, they held a *conference* at *St. Gilles*, but without success. When the Legate departed from him, the Count had him led with an *escort* as far as the *Rhone*, yet he was killed on the banks of that river, and the Count was suspected of having had a hand in it. In the year 1208, the Pope sent *Milo* <note>original: *Milon*</note> as Legate to *France* to incite King *Philip Augustus* to a war against the *Albigensians*. The *Count* <note>Raymond VI of Toulouse</note>, well seeing that he could not withstand all the power that came against him, betook himself to *Valence*, where the *Legate* <note>the Pope's official representative</note> was, to submit himself and prevent his ruin; yet he was received with the utmost contempt. They forced him to surrender his strongholds, they released his subjects from their oath of loyalty, and they made him appear in his bare shirt <note>a symbol of public humiliation and penance</note> in the Church of *St. Gilles*. There, he had to swear eternal submission to the *Roman See* <note>original: *Roomſchen Stoel*; the authority of the Pope</note> upon the *Sacrament* <note>the consecrated bread of the Eucharist</note>, whereupon the *Legate* scourged him with his own hands and, in the presence of a great number of people, dragged him along like a beast. This was not enough; the *Crusaders* besieged *Béziers*, captured it, and put everyone they found to death; the innocent, says a certain writer, perished with the guilty, the *Roman Catholics* with the *Albigensians*. Seven thousand *Romanists* <note>original: *Roomſchgezinden*; those loyal to the Catholic Church</note>, who had fled into a church, were murdered in cold blood. *Carcassonne* did not defend itself, and the inhabitants bargained for their lives on the condition that they must depart in their bare shirts and give the Count of *Béziers* as a hostage; yet he was soon put to death by the Count of *Montfort* <note>Simon de Montfort, the leader of the crusade</note>. After this "glorious" deed <note>the author uses "heerlyke" or "glorious" with heavy irony regarding the brutality</note>, and also because other lords were unwilling, the Count of *Montfort* was declared General of the *Crusaders*. *Castres* and the Castle of *Lavaur* were taken, and everyone found was hanged or burned, or had to perish by the sword.

The Pope gave fair words <note>polite but non-committal promises</note>, but the *Council of Lavaur* refused it. King *Peter* besieged *Muret*. The *Count of Montfort* threw himself into that place, made a sally, and a battle occurred there in which the King of *Aragon* fell, along with many nobles and 20,000 *Albigensians* <note>a dualist Christian sect in Southern France considered heretical by the Catholic Church</note>. This took place in the year 1213.

The *Count of Montfort*, General of the *Crusaders*, was declared lord and owner of the places he had captured by the *Council of Montpellier*, which was confirmed with a unanimous vote by the *Council of Lavaur*. With this, he believed he had everything he desired, but, as a historian says, God had not set His seal upon it <note>meaning it was not destined to last or lacked divine favor</note>, for the power of the *Crusaders*, gathered from various nations, began to vanish. The young *Count Raymond of Toulouse* gathered some people, with whom he took several cities, and the old *Count* entered *Toulouse*, where he was besieged by the *Count of Montfort*, who lost his life there; and thus the *Count of Toulouse* became free again and came into possession of his states. *Amaury* <note>original: *Aumarik*</note>, son of the *Count of Montfort*, offered his rights to

The Bishops accepted him and wanted the war to cease, but the Legate of the Pope saw it differently <note>the Legate, representing the Pope's direct authority, often took a more hardline stance than local clergy</note>. Thus the King's army broke camp, and everything had to yield before it. Finally *Avignon*, as the greatest bulwark of the *Albigensians*, was besieged. The King lost 25,000 men there, and would have had to break off the siege if the Legate had not known how to persuade the people of *Avignon* to allow him and some Prelates to enter the city to conduct an inquiry into their faith, promising by oath to come with only a few Prelates: but he brought soldiers in; and thus by this treachery the city was captured, the walls overturned, the moats filled in, several hundred houses torn down, and two hundred citizens taken along as hostages. Subsequently, only *Toulouse* remained, and it was spared until the next campaign. In the meantime, the King was poisoned by the Count of *Champagne*, lover of Queen *Blanche*, without her (who is so highly praised for her holiness by the *Roman writers*) wishing to have justice rightly exercised over it. All the *Albigensians* who were found there were burned; the other inhabitants were either beaten to death with clubs or slain by the edge of the sword. And to root out the *Albigensians* completely, it was decided to devastate everything surrounding *Toulouse*, which was carried out with great order. Every morning at the break of day, Mass was celebrated; immediately after, three types of men appeared, well-equipped for the work they were tasked to perform: some to tear down the houses, others to uproot the vineyards and trees, and the third to destroy the sown crops. *Toulouse* could not endure this and therefore fell once again under the *Roman Church*, and *Raymond the Younger*, then Count, fell into the power of the Queen; after which, to do penance, he was forced to wage war against the *Saracens* <note>a medieval term for Muslims, referring here to the Crusades in the Holy Land or Spain</note> for five years.

Now when the *Albigensians* were stripped of their supporters and protectors, and consequently weapons were no longer needed against them, they were nevertheless long raged against with *Edicts* and *Declarations*. *Louis IX* began his reign with a *Declaration* which he issued in the year 1229, whereby

# AL of the Heretics.

In this year, the Council of *Toulouse* assembled, in which the Inquisition—the honor of whose invention is disputed between Pope *Innocent* III and *St. Dominic*—was brought into full force, after which it was confirmed by several other Councils. In this Council of *Toulouse*, it is noteworthy that the <term>laity</term> <note>the common people, as distinguished from the clergy</note> were forbidden to possess the Old or New Testament; however, it was permitted that if someone, out of devotion, wished to have a Psalm-book or a <term>Breviary</term> <note>original: *Brevier*; a book of daily prayers and readings</note>, that is a book of hours, they must have it in the Latin language. Abbot *Fleury* <note>Claude Fleury (1640–1723), a French priest and ecclesiastical historian</note> notes in the 16th volume of his *Ecclesiastical History*, page 676, that this was the first prohibition he had found in that regard up to that time. But he seeks to excuse it.

The cruelties that were further committed against the *Albigensians* in the 13th and following centuries can be seen in the sentences passed against them, recorded by Mr. *Graverol* and published by Mr. *Limborch* <note>Philippus van Limborch (1633–1712), a Dutch theologian who published historical records of the Inquisition</note>, and are sufficiently documented by Roman Catholic writers. One reads of nothing but hanging and burning, and the <term>confiscation</term> <note>the state or church seizing private property</note> of goods for the benefit of the clergy. Even the dead were not spared. The young Count of *Toulouse* dearly wished to have the bones of his Father buried; however, although the King of *France* spoke in favor of it, it was not permitted to happen. What the *Inquisition* could not complete was accomplished through trickery and force, until the remaining *Albigensians* finally left the aforementioned regions—where they had first and most frequently resided—and were scattered into other lands, or retreated into the mountains or *Valleys* of *Provence*, *Dauphiné*, *Piedmont*, etc., gradually being called no longer *Albigensians*, but *Vaudois*, that is *Valdenzians* or *Waldensians* <note>A movement of "the Poor of Lyon" that sought a return to apostolic simplicity and moved into the Alpine valleys</note>. Emperor *Frederick II*, against whom the Pope waged war, issued harsh *Edicts* against the *Patarenes* <note>a term often used interchangeably for various medieval sects, including the Albigensians</note>—that is, the *Albigensians* and *Vaudois*, or *Waldensians*—to show that he was only against the Pope and not against the Church, of which he wished to be called a *Protector*.

See regarding all this more extensively *Ussher* <note>James Ussher (1581–1656), an influential Irish scholar and Archbishop</note>, *On the Perpetual Succession of the Church* <note>original Latin: *Ufferius de perpetua ſucceſſione Eccleſia*</note>, chapter 8; and *Basnage* <note>Jacques Basnage (1653–1723), a French Protestant historian</note> in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 24, chapters 3 to 10; and *Perrin*, *History of the Albigensians*.

### ALOGIANS, ALOGIANI, (The Wordless) <note>original Greek: *Ἄλογοι* (Alogoi)</note>
Heretics, named thus as if one said "without the Word" or "the Wordless," because they denied that *Jesus Christ* was the eternal Word <note>referring to the Greek concept of the *Logos*</note> of the Father. Because the Gospel and the Revelation of *John* overthrew their false propositions, they attributed these same works—if one may believe *Epiphanius* <note>an early Christian bishop and writer known for his works against heresies</note>—to the heretic *Cerinthus* <note>original: *Corinthus*; a 1st-century gnostic teacher</note>; although *John* [wrote] his Evan-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Christian sects from the previous section. <page-num>39</page-num>
<header>ON the Heretics.</header>

Gospel was written to oppose this heretic. *Theodotus*, a leather-dresser <note>or "tanner"</note> from *Byzantium*, was later the defender of these errors. *Epiphanius* is the only one of the ancient writers who mentions this sect of heretics, called *Alogians* <note>original Dutch: *Alogianen*; a group that rejected the Gospel of John and the Book of Revelation, often associating them with heretics</note>, whom he identifies as contemporaries of the *Cataphrygians* <note>Also known as Montanists, a prophetic movement from Phrygia in Asia Minor</note>. What he says about them seems very uncertain. *Tertullian, On the Prescription against Heretics, last chapter. Epiphanius, Heresies 51 & 54. Augustine, On Heresies, chapter 33. Eusebius, book 5, chapter 39. Baronius. Tillemont, etc.*

**ANGELICANS, ANGELICI**, were heretics so named because they worshiped the angels. It seems that this heresy already took its beginning in the time of the *Apostles*; however, it ceased with the *Melchisedecians* <note>A sect that held Melchizedek, the biblical priest-king, to be a divine power greater than Christ</note> around the year 180. *Augustine, Heresy 39* and *Epiphanius, Heresy 6* are of the opinion that they taught that the *World* was made by the *Angels*, or perhaps they were named so because they boasted of leading a life like that of the *Angels*, or because they came from a certain place on the other side of *Mesopotamia* called *Angelina*. However, it seems that there were two kinds of heretics of this name, since the latter ones mentioned by *Epiphanius* only arose in the 3rd century, and thus must be distinguished from the former. *Baronius, year of Christ 360, number 69.*

**ANGELITES**, certain heretics who followed *Sabellius* <note>A 3rd-century priest who taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were not distinct persons but merely different "modes" or "faces" of one God</note>; they took this name after a place in *Alexandria* called *Agelius* or *Angelius*, where they gathered together. *Nicephorus, book 18, chapter 49.*

# Alphabetical List of Names: AN

**ANTHROPOMORPHITES** <note>literally "those who give human form"</note>, named after one of the leaders of their sect called Audius <note>original Dutch: *Audeus*</note>, also called Audians <note>original Dutch: *Audeanen*</note>, were of the opinion that God had a physical or human form, according to which He had created man. They celebrated Easter in the Jewish manner <note>the practice of celebrating Easter on the 14th of Nisan, the same day as Passover</note>, and fell into the error of the Manichaeans <note>a dualistic religion teaching a struggle between light and darkness</note>, claiming that all things arose from darkness, fire, and water, yet that these things were not created by God. They separated themselves from the other Christian congregations under the pretext that there were many ungodly and impure people among them. The first followers of this Audius were the Egyptian monks. *Epiphanius, Heresies 70. Augustine, Heresies 50.*

**ANTIDICOMARIANITES** <note>literally "opponents of Mary"</note>, was the name given to those heretics <note>literally "spirits of error"</note> who denied the virginity of Mary and claimed that after the birth of the Lord, she bore children with Joseph. This error was introduced by one Helvidius around the end of the 4th century, which is why these heretics are also called Helvidians. *Epiphanius, Heresies 78. Augustine, Heresies 56 & 84. Jerome, Against Helvidius. Baronius, Year of Christ 373.*

**ANTINOMIANS** <note>literally "those against the law"</note>, heretics thus named because they rejected the Law <note>specifically the moral laws of the Old Testament</note>, considering it to be of no use under the Gospel. They say that good works are of no use for anything, and that evil works do not harm one's salvation. That God never punishes a country for its sins. That murder, adultery, drunken-

### ANTITACTS <note>From the Greek word for "Opponents"</note>, a certain kind of heretics in the 2nd century, sprouted from the *Gnostics*, who believed that *God* had made everything within Himself well and good, but that one of His *creatures* had sown weeds in the world and brought it about that evil appeared. Since they also considered this *creature* to be the author of the law, they thought they were doing well whenever they opposed the commandment in everything and did the opposite, which is why they were called *Antitacts*. In everything they followed their impulses, since according to their view, sins deserved reward rather than punishment: *Clement of Alexandria, book 3 of the Miscellanies <note>original Latin: Strom.</note>. Theodoret, Compendium of Heretical Fables <note>original Latin: Hæret. Fab.</note>, book 1, chapter 16. Augustine, On Heresies <note>original Latin: de Hær.</note>, chapter 18. Daneau in Commentary on this work <note>original Latin: Comment. ad h. l.</note>. Baronius, Annals <note>original Latin: annal.</note>, year 120. Ittig, On the Leading Heretics <note>original Latin: de Hæresi arch.</note>, section 2, chapter 17.*

<header>AN AP *of Heretics.* 43</header>

**ANTITRINITARIANS**, a name commonly given to all those who deny the **Holy Trinity**, especially the **Socinians** <note>Followers of the 16th-century Italian theologians Lelio and Fausto Sozzini, who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, who call themselves **Unitarians**. *Sandius* <note>Christopher Sandius (1644–1680), an Arian historian</note> published a *Library of the Antitrinitarians* <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Antitrinitariorum*</note>, containing a list of all the works of the **Unitarians**.

**APELLITES**, certain heretics of the 2nd century and disciples of **Apelles**, who was himself a disciple of **Marcion** <note>A famous early Christian heretic who rejected the Old Testament</note>. He was of the opinion that there were two Gods: one good and one evil, of which the first <note>the evil one, according to his early view</note> had created the **world** and given the **law**; the other was the founder of the **Gospel** and the **Redeemer** of the world. He held this error in common with his master **Marcion**; however, having been expelled from his assembly due to some carnal dealings, he devised another heresy, or rather taught the same from **Philumena**, a possessed girl whom he claimed had an inspiration from the **Holy Spirit**. He then still proposed two Gods, but suggested that the one who was good and the most prominent had also made the evil one. He rejected the **law** and the **Prophets**, and attributed to **Christ** an aerial body <note>meaning a body made of the elements of the air rather than human flesh</note>, of which, when He ascended to Heaven, He had returned to each element its portion. He also denied the bodily resurrection. The Church Fathers strongly refuted these errors. *Epiphanius, Heresies 44. Augustine, chapter 23, On Heresies. Daneau on this passage. Tertullian, On the Prescription of Heretics, chapters 30, 31. Eusebius, book 3, Ecclesiastical History, chapter 13. Baronius, Annals, year 146. Ittig, On the Heresiarchs.* <note>These references cite various early Christian scholars and historians who chronicled and argued against these sects.</note>

# APOLLINARIANS

**APOLLINARIANS** were the name given to the followers of the heretic *Apollinarius*. He was a native of *Laodicea*, a son of *Apollinaris* the Elder, and a student of *Epiphanius* the *Sophist*. He first taught *Logic* <note>original Dutch: *Redeneerkunde*</note>, later became *Rector*, and finally *Bishop of Laodicea*. He flourished around the year 370 and was at first devoted to the orthodox Church, but later fell into certain errors. *George* <note>original: *Georgius*</note>, *Bishop of Laodicea*, is said to have given cause for this; he was an *Arian* <note>A follower of Arius, who taught that Jesus was a created being and not equal to God the Father</note> who persecuted *Apollinarius* for being a friend of *Athanasius* <note>The Bishop of Alexandria and chief defender of Trinitarian orthodoxy against Arianism</note>.

Because of this, *Apollinarius* was said to have been moved to found his own *Sect*, as he taught that Christ had brought his *human nature* with him from Heaven, and that He had possessed a soul of His own which had been constantly united with His essence; yet, he claimed He no longer possessed it, but the *Godhead* <note>The divine nature of Christ</note> had taken the place of the soul. He taught that Christ was mortal and had suffered according to the *Godhead*; also that the *Godhead* had died and was raised again by the Father. 

When he began to spread this doctrine is uncertain, though it is likely that he was not its first inventor, as some of these doctrines were already rejected at the *Council of Chalcedon* <note>The text cites the year 362; however, the Great Council of Chalcedon occurred in 451. The author likely refers to the Council of Alexandria held in 362, which addressed similar Christological issues</note> in the year 362. *Apollinarius* was also condemned for this, particularly at the *Council of Rome*, which was held in the year 373, or as others would have it, in the year 377 or 378. However, he remained steadfast in his opinion and died under the Emperor *Theodosius*. He was a learned man and wrote extensively, such as 

<summary>This page details the life and heterodox teachings of Apollinarius of Laodicea, who founded the Apollinarian sect after departing from orthodox doctrine. It outlines his specific belief that the divine Word replaced the human soul in Christ, and notes his subsequent condemnation by Church councils.</summary>
<keywords>Apollinarians, Apollinarius of Laodicea, Christology, Heresy, Arianism, Athanasius, Council of Rome, Council of Chalcedon, 4th Century Church History</keywords>

**APOSTOLICS** or **APOTACTICS** <note>Greek: *Apotaktikoi*, meaning "renouncers"</note>, a certain kind of heretic emerging from the sects of the *Encratites* <note>An ascetic group that practiced extreme self-denial</note> and *Cathari* <note>A name meaning "the pure," used by several groups claiming moral rigor</note> around the year 260; although the time when they arose is very uncertain. They bore this name because they made vows never to marry and because they despised all possessions and riches. *Epiphanius* <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his catalog of eighty heresies</note> notes that they used certain *Apocryphal Books* of *Andrew* and *Thomas*. *Epiphanius, Heresy 61; Augustine, Heresy 40; Baronius.*

**AQUARIANS** or **HYDROPARASTATANS** <note>Greek: *Hydroparastatai*, "those who offer water"</note> were heretics in the 3rd century, around the year 357, who used water instead of wine in the Lord’s Supper <note>Dutch: *Avondmaal*</note> because they believed that wine was a harmful creature and the cause of many sins. Some consider them to be the same as the *Encratites*, or that they were descended from them. *Cyprian* <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage and martyr</note> also mentions some Christians who, out of fear and to escape persecution, used water when they met at night or in the early morning to hold the Lord’s Supper, so that through the smell of the

**ARABICS**, or **ARABICI**, Heretics <note>A term used by the author for groups deviating from established church doctrine</note> who arose in the 3rd century. They taught that the souls of men died with their bodies and would be raised again with them. It is not known who the first inventor of this error was; and *Prateolus* <note>Gabriel Prateolus, a 16th-century controversialist</note> only notes that it began to appear around the year 207, during the Papacy of *Zephyrinus* <note>Pope from 199–217 AD</note> and under the Empire of *Severus* <note>Septimius Severus, Roman Emperor</note>. The second Council of Arabia was held against these heretics, who renounced their errors and made a profession of the orthodox faith. *Augustine, On Heresies, Chapter 85; Eusebius, Book 6 of History; Nicephorus, Book 5, Chapter 23; Prateolus, Baronius.*

**ARIANS** were heretics, thus named after their founder, *Arius* <note>A priest in Alexandria whose teachings led to the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD</note>. This heresy arose in the fourth century and caused a great stir in the world, as it had many followers and concerned the weighty article of faith regarding the eternal Divinity and the <term>consubstantiality</term> <note>Latin: *consubstantialitas*; Greek: *homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance." This refers to the belief that the Son is of the exact same essence as the Father</note> of God the Son, the second Person in the Holy Trinity, which was denied by these heretics. <header>History of the Heretics. 47</header>

The beginning of **Arianism** was brought about by certain differences that *Alexander*, Bishop of Alexandria, had with *Arius*, one of his Priests, regarding the Divinity of *Jesus Christ*. To properly understand what these differences consisted of, one must necessarily examine the matter from a higher perspective and investigate the foundation of the *philosophy* used among Christians at that time, and how it had been introduced. This is a necessary digression, as will become apparent in what follows.

There has been no philosophy that has made itself so famous as that of *Plato*, and there have been no books read with as much pleasure as his, whether due to the *subjects* and the elevated thoughts found within them, or due to the purity and sublimity of their style. He was born under the reign of *Artaxerxes*, nicknamed *Longhand* <note>Artaxerxes I, often called *Longimanus* in Latin texts</note>, 426 years before Christ, and died at 80 years old. This was during the time when *Philip of Macedon*, who made all of Greece fear him, had a son named *Alexander* <note>Alexander the Great</note> who made himself master of *Asia*. Because his successors divided that realm among themselves, one can easily believe that the Greek sciences established themselves there along with their government and customs. *Ptolemy*, the son of *Lagus* <note>Ptolemy I Soter, founder of the Ptolemaic Kingdom in Egypt</note>, one of the successors of *Alexander*, collected as many books as possible in his Library in *Alexandria*, and he lured several scholars from Greece to that place.

<summary>This page explains that understanding the Arian controversy requires looking at the influence of Platonic philosophy in Alexandria, tracing the spread of Greek thought from the conquests of Alexander the Great to the establishment of the Great Library by Ptolemy.</summary>

<keywords>Arianism, Arius, Alexander of Alexandria, Plato, Platonic philosophy, Artaxerxes Longimanus, Alexander the Great, Ptolemy I Lagus, Library of Alexandria, Greek philosophy in Christianity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spread of Greek philosophy, specifically how Ptolemy I and his successors in Egypt and Syria fostered a culture of learning that ensured the survival and influence of Plato’s works.</meta>

# Alphabetical Name List AR

...wards. He was himself a learned Man, and did not forget to instill a love for the *Sciences* <note>original: *Wetenschappen*, referring to the liberal arts and academic learning</note> in his *Children*. His Son *Philadelphus* <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus</note> followed here in his Father's footsteps. The Kings of *Syria* also seem to have practiced and promoted the *Sciences*, as *Suidas* <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient world</note> recounts that *Euphorion* of *Chalcis* in *Euboea*, a Poet and Philosopher, was the *Librarian* of *Antiochus the Great*, 200 years before *Christ*. *Plato* was by then much too famous, and his Works held in too high regard, for them not to have been placed in these Libraries. One may also believe that *Asia*, which was then filled with Greek Philosophers, had no lack of *Platonists*.

Among the views of *Plato*, none are more noteworthy than those he held regarding *Divinity*, *Pre-existence*, and the *Immortality of the Soul*. He believed that there was but one *supreme, spiritual, and invisible God*, whom he calls *the Being*, or *Being itself*, *the Good itself*, the *Father* and the *Origin* of all *Beings*, etc. Beneath this supreme God, he placed a lesser *Being*, which he calls *the Word* <note>original: Λόγος (*Logos*)</note>, the *Governor* of present and future affairs, the *Creator* of the universe, etc. Finally, he recognized a third *Being*, which he calls *the Spirit*, or the *Soul of the World*. He added further that the first was the *Father* of the second, and that the second had brought forth the third. One may consult his *Timaeus* <note>One of Plato's most influential dialogues, dealing with the nature of the physical world and the creator</note> regarding this, to which one must add his second and sixth Letter. In the second, which was addressed to *Di-*

### AR of the Heretics

[Di]onysius <note>Likely Dionysius the Younger, the tyrant of Syracuse, who was a pupil of Plato</note> is directed, who complained that Plato had not taught him enough regarding the first nature, or the first *Being*, this Philosopher speaks thus:

> “Everything is around the King of all things, and everything is for his sake; he is the cause of all good things: things of the second order are around the second: things of the third are around the third.” <note>original: "Alles is rondom den Koning van alle dingen..." This quote is from Plato’s Second Letter, a text that was highly influential in the development of the doctrine of the Trinity</note>

He calls that a mystery <note>original: *verborgentheid*</note>, and forbids *Dionysius* from speaking of it before the ignorant, commands him to burn his *Letter* as soon as he has read it, and declares that he will never write on this subject. In his 6th *Letter* he recommends to *Hermias*, to *Erastus*, and to *Coriscus*, swearing to take as witnesses the God who is the ruler of present and future things, and the Lord, who is the Father of this ruler and of this cause. The darkness <note>meaning the intentional obscurity or "vague language"</note> which he assumes here, out of fear of bringing the wrath of the superstitious people upon his head, is the reason why one cannot understand what he wants to say except by comparing all the passages where he speaks of the *Divinity*, and by examining his *Interpreters* and *Disciples*. See here how one of them, namely *Hierocles* <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher of the 5th century AD</note> in *On Providence* <note>original: *de Provid.* (De Providentia)</note> as cited in *Photius’s Codex 251* <note>Photius was a 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople whose *Bibliotheca* or *Codex* preserved summaries of many lost ancient books</note>, explains the thoughts of his Master:

> “Plato believed that God the Creator maintains the visible and invisible world, which was brought forth from nothing; that his will is sufficient to cause beings to exist; that he, through the joining of a corporeal [nature], has made a perfect world,”

### Alphabetical Register of Heretics

D 2

passages of his *Enneads*, especially *En.* 5. lib. 1. cap. 3. to 8. but primarily in the book, named *Of the Three Hypostases* <note>original: *beſtaanelykheden*; referring to Plotinus’s treatise "On the Three Principal Hypostases," Ennead V.1</note>, which are the causes of all things. See here what his doctrine amounts to. 1. There are three causes: *The Being* <note>Refers to "The One," the supreme, transcendent source in Neoplatonism</note>, *the Mind* <note>original: *de Geeſt*; representing the *Nous* or Divine Intellect</note>, or *the word of the Being*, and *the World Soul*, which is the *Word* of the *Mind*. There is, according to his proposition, also a word of the *World Soul*, but that is a faint <note>Greek: *amydros* (ἀμυδρὸς)</note> word. 2. The *Being* has begotten the *Word*, not by an act of his will, or by a decree, but by his nature, just as *Fire* begets *Heat*, or as the *Sun* produces *Light*. The *Word* has also begotten the *World Soul*, and can in that respect be called *Father*. 3. These three hypostases <note>original: *beſtaanelykheden*</note> differ in number, although there is a very close union between them, which is why one can say at the same time that they are distinct, and that they are the same thing. The first is more excellent than the second, and the second than the third. 4. The expressions of *Plotinus* are very remarkable. 1. He not only calls the nature of the *Being*, of the *Word*, and of the *World Soul* *essence* <note>Greek: *ousia* (οὐσία)</note>, after *Plato*, but he even makes use of the expression *matter* <note>Greek: *hylē* (ὕλη); in Neoplatonism, "intelligible matter" is a concept used to describe the underlying principle of the higher realms</note>, and says that the *matter* of the one is more perfect than that of the other. When he claims that *Parmenides* <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher</note> had said before *Plato* that there were three causes, he expresses himself in this way: *Parmenides* is also of the opinion of the three natures. 2. One sees that the word *hypostasis* <note>original: *beſtaanelykheid*; Greek: *hypostasis* (ὑπόστασις)</note> signifies two things

Just as he says that the *Word* is the *Father* of the *Soul*, he also says that the *Word* begets and makes the *Soul*: for it must be noted that *Plato* and his pupils use the words *beget*, *make*, *procreate*, etc., interchangeably in this matter, and that *begotten* and *made* are here the same thing in their speech. One need only read the *Timaeus* of *Plato*. 4. *Plotinus* says that the *Father* and the *Word* are one and the same thing, because they coexist, and the one does not leave the other. He says that the *Supreme Being*, whose essence consists in existing, has in a quite singular way, through his nature, begotten the *Mind* <note>original: *Geeft* (archaic for *Geest*); refers to the *Nous* or Divine Intellect</note>, and that it cannot be without him, any more than a luminous body can be without light. The *Mind* on its part, whose essence consists in eternally having a living conception of the *Being*, cannot exist <note>original Greek: ὑποστᾶναι (*hypostanai*)</note> without it. They cannot be separated <note>original Greek: χωρισθῆναι (*chōristhēnai*)</note>, because there is nothing between them, just as there is nothing between the *Mind* and the *Soul*. 5. He says that that which is begotten is similar <note>original Greek: ὁμοῖόν ἐστι (*homoion esti*)</note> to its cause, just as light is similar to the *Sun*. 6.
