St. Cyril of Alexandria, in his 8th book against *Julian* <note>Julian the Apostate, the Roman Emperor who tried to revive paganism</note>, cites a passage by *Porphyry*, from his 3rd book of the *Philosophical History*, through which one sees that the *Platonists* dispute among themselves whether there could be more than three *existences* <note>original: *beſtaanlykheden*; referring to the Three Hypostases</note> in the *Godhead*. "Plato (*says Porphyry*) taught that the *Divine being* could extend itself to three *existences*, namely the *Supreme Godhead*, or the *Good itself*; after that the *Creator* who is the second; and the *Soul of the World*, which is the third, etc. But there are those who maintain that one should not count *goodness itself* among the things it has produced, and that, being of a perfect *simplicity* and subject to no *accidents* <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are properties that are not essential to a thing's nature</note>, it has no fellowship with anything, so that one must begin to count the *Trinity* <note>original Greek: τὴν τριάδα (*tēn triada*)</note> starting from the *Spirit* <note>Divine Mind</note>."

The master of *Porphyry* [Plotinus], whom we have already mentioned, seems meanwhile to say (*Ennead 5, book 8, chapter 12*) that there could be more than three *existences*, in these remarkable words: "God has fathered an excellent *Being*, and has begotten everything within himself. This birth was within him without pain, for taking pleasure in what he begot, and finding his productions good, he kept them all within himself, tempering his luster with theirs. Those which remained there, more excellent..."

One can see through this the subtlety with which the *Platonists* handle this matter, and the expressions which they used. But one must pay close attention to two things to have a good idea of their sentiment. The first is, that one must not always assume that they had a distinct and clear idea of what they wanted to say, or that they saw all the consequences of their views. Thus, it would perhaps be fruitless to try to draw from their writings a clear idea of their sentiment regarding the three primary causes of all things, because they per-

...perhaps they did not even clearly understand what they were saying. At the very least, their style on this occasion is so different from that which one perceives in those places where they speak of things they were capable of knowing, that one plainly sees they did not comprehend the matter of the three *Primary Causes* <note>Dutch: *Hoofdoorzaaken*. In Neoplatonism, this usually refers to the three fundamental principles or "hypostases": The One, the Intellect (Nous), and the Soul.</note>, as they have known how to express an endless number of other things at once in a clear and elegant manner.

The second matter to which one must pay attention is that, in such a difficult subject, one must be satisfied with what they positively state, without attempting to draw far-fetched consequences from their principles, which can only be half-understood. Otherwise, one would run the risk of attributing thoughts to them which they never held.

One should not even attempt to reconcile the contradictions in such an abstract Subject that might seem to be found in their teachings, nor conclude that they could not have understood matters in any certain way because, if that were the case, they would have contradicted themselves. It was the custom of these *Philosophers* <note>Dutch: *Philosophen*</note> that they seemed to contradict themselves in a certain way by using the same expressions in different senses. Moreover, it is easy to understand that they might sometimes have been able to contradict themselves in a matter of which they had no distinct idea <note>Dutch: *onderſcheiden denkbeeld*—a clear and discrete concept</note>.

These two remarks were necessary to anticipate the questions which one would—

...could do regarding these subjects, and to make it understood that in giving a *History* of these *Dogmas* <note>Dutch: *Leerstukken*—foundational religious or philosophical tenets</note>, one must limit oneself solely to the *propositions* and *expressions of the Author* of whom one speaks.

A second *Dogma of the Platonists*, which has caused much stir, is that of the *pre-existence of souls in the sublunar places* <note>In ancient cosmology, the "sublunar" realm was the region of the cosmos below the moon, subject to change and decay, as opposed to the eternal celestial spheres.</note> (see the *Timaeus of Plato*), of the misdeeds they might have committed there, of their banishment from this happy residence to come dwell in bodies that are arranged in a different manner according to the various merits of these Souls; and finally of the *return to the places* from where they took their *origin*.

The Kings of *Egypt* and *Syria*, having brought the sciences of the Greeks into *Asia*, the *Jews*, who were numerous in these two Kingdoms and were obliged to interact with them, learned their views from them, and made no difficulty in embracing those which did not seem to conflict with their Religion. Since their books <note>Referring to the Jewish scriptures</note> contained nothing inconsistent with various *Dogmas of the Platonists*, they thought that these Dogmas could be true, and adopted them more readily because they thought they could thereby defend their religion against the *Heathens* <note>Dutch: *Heidenen*—non-monotheistic practitioners, often translated as Pagans.</note>, and make it more easily palatable to them. *Plato* asserts everywhere the *unity of the Supreme Being*, without however denying that there are other *Beings* which one can call *Gods*, namely the *Angels*, the

The same author made use of the expression <term>Logos</term> <note>Greek: λόγος; meaning "word," "reason," or "divine intellect." In Christian and Jewish-Platonic philosophy, it refers to the bridge between God and the material world.</note>—"Word"—in several places where Plato would have used it if he had intended to convey the same meaning. Carrying a sharp sword, (namely) your unfeigned commandment; and standing, it filled all things with death; it reached indeed unto heaven, but it also walked upon the earth.*

In the 2nd verse of the 9th chapter, he says that God made all things through his Word. One cannot say that this was the only Jew who spoke in this way, since the writings of Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, who lived a short time after Christ, are filled with similar expressions, as various scholars have noted. It is known that this writer followed Plato so well that he is called the "Plato of the Jews."

He believed in a single *Supreme God*, just as the other *Jews* did, whom he called <term>the Being</term> <note>Original Greek: $\tau o$ $ò\nu$ (*to on*); referring to the self-existent, absolute nature of God.</note>, or the *Being* par excellence; but he also recognized a *Divine nature*, which he calls the <term>Logos</term> <note>Original Greek: $\lambda\acute{o}\gamma o\varsigma$; meaning the Word or Divine Reason.</note>—the *word*—just as *Plato* did; and another, which he also calls the *Soul of the World*. It is known that *Amelius*, a *Platonic Philosopher*, having read the beginning of this *Gospel*, remarked that this *Apostle* spoke just like *Plato*. Indeed, this *Philosopher* could have said, according to his own principles: *In the beginning was the word in God* (the phrase <note>Original Greek: $\pi\rho\grave{o}\varsigma$ $\Theta\epsilon\grave{o}\nu$ (*pros Theon*), usually translated as "with God."</note> is the same as <note>Original Greek: $\pi\alpha\rho\grave{a}$ $\Theta\epsilon\tilde{\omega}$ (*para Theo*), meaning "alongside God," which Plotinus used</note> that which *Plotinus* employed) *and it was God; the same has made all things, which is the life, and the light of men*, etc.

> become subject to the same weaknesses
> as we, has taught us through himself.”

*Clement of Alexandria* also believed that *Plato* had known the *Holy Trinity*. *Origen* against *Celsus* <note>Origen’s *Contra Celsum* is one of the most important works of early Christian apologetics.</note> does not deny that *Plato* spoke the truth (*book 6, pages 276 and 280*) when speaking of God and of his Son; he only asserts that he did not make the use of his enlightenment that he should have. He does not say that the foundation of the *Christian doctrine* is different therein from that of *Plato*, but that this *Philosopher* had learned the same from the *Jews* <note>A common argument among early Christian scholars was that Greek philosophers had traveled to Egypt and read the books of Moses.</note>. *Constantine* in his oration to the Saints chapter 9, after he praised *Plato* as the first *Philosopher* who brought men to the contemplation of intellectual matters, continues as follows:

> “He has spoken of the first God,
> who is above all being, in which he
> did well. He has subjected another to
> him, and has [placed] two *Beings* in...”

wonder that since the time of the *Apostles*, various opinions have existed on this subject. Thus, the *Ebionites* <note>An early Jewish Christian sect that emphasized Jewish law and viewed Jesus as a human prophet rather than divine.</note>, who appear to have been from *Judea*, were accused of denying the pre-existence of the divinity of *Christ*, believing that he was but a single man. These *Ebionites* persisted for a long time, as not only *Justin Martyr* and *Irenaeus* speak of them, but *Jerome* <note>original: "Hieronimus"</note> seems to indicate that there were still some in the time when he wrote. It is asserted that *Artemon*, under the Empire of *Severus*, and *Paul of Samosata*, Bishop of *Antioch*, under that of *Aurelianus*, maintained the same opinions.

*Cerinthus*, on the contrary, believed in the *pre-existence* of the *Word* <note>The Logos, or the divine reason of God.</note>, which he called the *Christ*, and claimed that it had descended upon *Jesus* in and under the form of a *dove* when he was baptized, and that it ascended back to heaven when he was crucified. In truth, one can hardly be certain that these were actually the thoughts of these *Heretics* <note>original: "Ketters"; used here in the historical sense of those holding beliefs contrary to established Church doctrine.</note>, because nothing of their own writings remains, and one cannot fully trust those who speak of them with nothing but loathing; for their great zeal may have prevented them from understanding them well. This is a remark one must make regarding all the *Ancient Heretics*, whose views are known to us only through the writings of their opponents.

### Alphabetical List of Names: AR

The fathers of the Council of Antioch said that the *Father* and the *Son* were not <term>consubstantial</term> <note>Dutch: "medewezendlyk." This refers to the theological concept of being of the exact same essence or substance.</note> in the same sense that one says two coins struck from the same metal are consubstantial. This is because such coins presuppose a pre-existing material from which they both were made, whereas the *Father* and the *Son* do not presuppose a shared, pre-existing substance. *Paul of Samosata* argued that if the *Son* were not a created *God*, one would have to assume he is of the same kind of essence as that of the *Father*, and thus there would have been a single substance prior to both from which they both would have been formed. *Athanasius*, in his *Book on the Councils of Ariminum and Seleucia* <note>original: "lib. de Syn. Arim, &c. Seleuc. T. 1. pag. 919. seq."</note>, asserts that the council at *Antioch* only condemned the use of the term <term>homoousios</term> <note>original: "Homousius"; from the Greek "ὁμοούσιος," meaning "of the same substance."</note> insofar as it might contain the idea of a substance that existed before the entities which are called *consubstantial*.

These are the primary heretical views regarding the divinity of *Jesus Christ* that appeared before the Council of Nicaea. As for the Church Fathers who are considered orthodox, they did not deviate from the expressions of the *Platonists*. Just as the Platonists sometimes said that the *Word* <note>The "Logos," referring to the Second Person of the Trinity.</note> is distinct from the *Supreme Being*, and at other times that they are but *one*, the Fathers spoke using the same expressions. The *Platonists* said that the *Father* could not exist without the *Son*, nor the *Son*...

All that one can do in this instance is to recount the *idioms* <note>original: "spreekwyfen" – here referring to rhetorical figures or specific ways of speaking used to describe divine concepts.</note> which they employed, so that one may see how people expressed themselves on this subject in former times. Scholars have meanwhile gone to great lengths to explain the passages of the *Fathers* <note>The "Church Fathers," early influential Christian theologians and leaders.</note> who lived before the *Council of Nicaea* <note>The Council of Nicaea (325 AD) was the first ecumenical council of the Christian Church, called to resolve the Arian controversy and define the relationship between God the Father and God the Son.</note>, without considering that all their explanations are useless; since the *Fathers*, by admitting that what they said was *incomprehensible*, at the same time admitted that they attached no clear concept to the expressions they used, unless they were very *general and confused* ideas.

If people had not gone further than this, there would never have been so many disputes over the views of the *Ancients* regarding this *mystery* <term>mystery</term> <note>Dutch: "verborgentheid" – in this theological context, it refers to a truth that exceeds human understanding, specifically the nature of the Trinity.</note>; because people do not dispute so much over the expressions they used, as over the ideas they attached to them, about which no firm conclusion could be made. Sometimes they employ idioms that seem to agree perfectly with those used later on: but in other places in their works, one finds expressions that seem to negate what they had previously said; so that one can form no clear idea of what they thought. *Lactantius* <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author who served as an advisor to the first Christian Roman emperor, Constantine I.</note>, for example, answers the *Pagans* <note>original: "Heidenen" – referring to followers of traditional Roman and Greek polytheistic religions.</note> in this way, who asked the *Christians* how they could say they recognized only one God, while they [gave] this name...

Each of them brings forward their own passages which, when examined separately, seem to speak in favor of their view; but when one compares these passages with one another, one cannot understand how the same persons could have spoken so differently. One finds in these comparisons their expressions to be so *obscure*, and so full of *probable* or *essential contradictions*, that one feels moved to believe that the *Fathers* would have done much better to keep to the expressions of the *Apostles*, and to admit that they did not understand them, than to involve themselves in so many difficulties by wishing to explain them.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To give another example of this, through which one shall see that the expressions of the *Fathers* are only capable of producing uncommonly confused ideas, or those which are contrary to those which all *Christians* today receive, *Tertullian* <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD).</note> in chapter 31, after having said in his defense <note>Referring to his *Apology* (Apologeticus).</note> that the nature of the *Word* is spiritual, adds to it: <note>original: "Hunc ex Deo prolatum dicimus, & prolatione Generatum, et idcirco filium & Deum dictum ex unitate substantiæ; nam & Deus & spiritus est."</note> *We say that this one was brought forth from God, and Generated by bringing forth, and for that reason called Son and God from the unity of substance; for He is both God and Spirit.* But what *Generated by bringing forth* <note>The text ends here with the start of the Latin word "Genitus".</note>

# of the Heretics.

„ ...was not separated from his origin, but he has gone out from it. ” These words of *Tertullian* do not seem at first glance to correspond with the opinion of *Arius*, but at their root, they contain nothing clear; for one could have asked *Tertullian* whether, through this *Prolation* <term>Prolation: A theological term (from the Latin *prolatio*) meaning 'to bring forth' or 'emit.' It was used to describe how the Word came from the Father.</term> of which he speaks, *the Word* existed in the same way the light of a torch, lit from another torch, exists as soon as it is ignited? If he had said yes, one would have answered that, to speak in the strictest sense, there would then be two Gods, because two spirits—though perfectly similar and closely united—are still two spirits. If such is the case, and the second Spirit was not formed from the same substance <term>substance: The author uses 'zelfstandigheid,' referring to the divine essence or *ousia*. The debate is whether the Son is the 'same' essence or just a 'similar' one.</term> in number as that of the first, then one could say with *Arius* that he was taken from nothing; in this respect, there would be nothing more than a difference in words between *Arius* and *Tertullian*. 

But if one answers on behalf of *Tertullian* that his comparison is not good, one must ask why he used a comparison that could lead to error, especially after having previously said that he held the opinion of *Plato* regarding *the Word*? If he understood that the Father brought forth [the Son] within his own substance without multiplying it (a definition by which one can call the substance of the Father 'the Son'), why then does he say *Spirit from Spirit, God from God*? <note>original Latin: "spiritus ex spiritu, ex Deo Deus"</note> for at the root, to properly

...speak, the *Father* has not brought forth a *Spirit*, or a *God*, but a mode of being within his own substance.

Furthermore, one must note that this [ambiguity] does not belong to *Tertullian* alone, but to *Justin Martyr*, and to a great number of other *Fathers* before and after the *Council of Nicaea*; and there is no passage more powerful than this one, in which one nevertheless sees the ambiguity.

The *Fathers* also made use of the expression *subsistence* <term>subsistence: Translated from 'bestaanlykheid,' likely referring to the Greek 'hypostasis'—the distinct personhood of the Father, Son, or Holy Spirit.</term> just like the *Platonists*, in two different senses: sometimes for *subsistence* taken in an isolated manner, and sometimes for the *thing* itself which exists. The ambiguity of these expressions, and those of the words "one" and "various"—which, as has been seen, are taken sometimes in the *descriptive* and sometimes in the *numerical* sense <note>The author distinguishes between 'descriptive unity' (being one in character or purpose) and 'numerical unity' (being one single entity/being).</note>—have caused great differences among the *Fathers*, as *Petavius*, *Curcellius*, *Huetius*, and *Bull* <note>Dionysius Petavius, Etienne de Courcelles, Pierre Daniel Huet, and George Bull were prominent 17th-century theologians and scholars who debated the orthodoxy of the pre-Nicene Church Fathers.</note> have shown. But it is well to note one thing, namely, that *Bull*, who has written at length on the subject, said nothing of *numerical* and *descriptive unity*, without which one cannot understand what the *Fathers* intend to say, nor conclude anything from it against the *Heretics*. Nevertheless, when they say that there are three *subsistences*, or three *beings*, or three *natures*, he always interprets it as if they said that there are three *definitions* in one *being* that is numerically one. He presupposes that the *ousia* <gloss>original: "οὐσία" — Essence or Being</gloss> and *physis* <gloss>original: "φύσις" — Nature</gloss> signify the modes of *existence* of a numerically single Being, only in order to

On the contrary, he assumes, for the same reasons, that when the *Fathers* deny that there are three subsistences <term>subsistences: 'bestaanlykheden', often used to translate the Greek 'hypostases'—the distinct persons of the Trinity.</term>, they do not only mean to say that there are not three *Beings* <note>original: "Wezens"</note> of different kinds, but also that there are not three in number. But others will deny that there is a single passage where the words "nature" and "Beings" can be taken for what is currently called *Personhood*, that is to say, for a specific definition; and that it appears, from the passages he cites, that the *Fathers* believed in a numerical *Unity* <note>original: "tellende Eenheid"; the concept that God is one single entity rather than three distinct beings acting as one.</note>.

This was the state of opinions within the *Christian Church* when the disputes of *Arius* disturbed it, from which it can be seen that it was not difficult for both parties to invoke the authority of the *Ancients*, whose ambiguous expressions could be interpreted in various ways. The obscurity of the subject, the vain cleverness of the human mind—which wants to know everything—the desire to appear competent, and the passion that mingles with all disputes, gave birth to differences that miserably tore *Christendom* apart for a long time.

# AR of the Heretics.

...of an affected <note>original: "gemaakte"; meaning artificial or put-on for display</note> religiosity, and of having voluntarily prescribed for himself a way of life that was suited to making himself popular among the people. *Arius* also had as an enemy one of his fellow priests from *Alexandria*, named *Alexander*, and surnamed *Baucalas*. (*Philostorgius, Book 1, Chapter 4.*) This man joined with *Meletius* to complain to the Bishop of *Alexandria* that *Arius* was sowing a new doctrine concerning the Divinity of *Jesus Christ*. He was able to spread his views more easily because, having a specific church in *Alexandria* that was entrusted to his care, he preached there whatever seemed good to him. He drew such a large number of people to his views that there were seven hundred *Monks* who had embraced them, and consequently a much larger multitude of the common people.

Bishop *Alexander* thought that in a matter where one could easily be ambiguous, both parties should be allowed to explain themselves, so that it would appear he had united them through conviction rather than by force. He brought both parties together to enter into negotiations, asking them for an explanation of a passage in the Scripture in the presence of the clergy of his church; but neither one nor the other would yield, and in this negotiation, they sought only to be victorious. The opponents of *Arius* asserted that the Son is of the same essence (consubstantial <note>original Greek: ὁμοούσιος (homoousios)</note>) as the Father, and that He is eternal just like Him; and *Arius* maintained that the generation <note>original: "Generatie"; referring to the "begetting" of the Son by the Father</note> signified a begin-

He then commanded this Priest to believe the same and to abandon the opposing view.

But since people seldom surrender to such commands, *Arius* remained firm in his opinion, along with several *Bishops* and the members of the clergy who had agreed with it. *Alexander*, angry because he was not obeyed, excommunicated <note>original: "in den ban"; to be placed under a formal ecclesiastical ban or anathema</note> him and all those who were of his faction, and forced him to leave *Alexandria*. Among others, there were five Priests from this City, and as many Deacons from the same Church, besides some *Bishops* from *Egypt*, such as *Secundus* and *Theonas*. <note>Secundus of Ptolemais and Theonas of Marmarica were the only two bishops who ultimately refused to sign the Nicene Creed in support of Arius.</note> They were joined by many people, some of whom indeed agreed with the opinion of *Arius*, and others who believed that he had been condemned with too much pride, without a precise investigation of the dispute.

After this severity, both Parties attempted, through letters which they sent everywhere, to justify their views and their conduct. They not only presented their reasons, but they also tried each to make his opponent hated by the consequences they drew from his opinion, and by attributing strange expressions to him. The discord meanwhile increased so powerfully that in some places it erupted into riot, and people even threw down the statues of the Emperor, who seemed to want to favor the *Arians* <note>Followers of Arius, who taught that Jesus was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father.</note> because he desired that they be tolerated. There was also still the dispute over the *Easter festival* <note>The "Quartodeciman" controversy: a debate over whether Easter should be celebrated on the 14th of the Jewish month of Nisan (the same day as Passover) or always on a Sunday.</note>; some denied that it should be celebrated at the same time as the Jews, and others maintained as much; but this dispute had not brought about a schism like *Arianism*.

*Constantine*, seeing that his letters had been in vain, believed that there was no better means to remove these differences than to summon a *Council* from all the Provinces of the Roman Empire. It was perhaps *Hosius* <note>Hosius of Corduba, a prominent Spanish bishop and influential advisor to Constantine.</note> who gave him this advice: at least if one may believe *Philostorgus* <note>An Arian church historian whose work often provides an alternative perspective to the orthodox accounts.</note> *Book 1, Chapter 7*, the Bishop of *Alexandria* having gone to *Nicomedia*, gathered there some Bishops of his opinion, with whom *Hosius* and he prepared themselves to devise the means to have their opinion established and *Arius* condemned; and a short time thereafter the Emperor summoned a *Council* at *Nicaea*, a City of *Bithynia* <note>A region in what is now northwestern Turkey.</note> *Eusebius, Life of Constantine, Book 3, Chapter 6.* This was in the year 325; and so that nothing might restrain the Bishops from coming there, the Emperor had them provided with the necessities for the journey at his own expense. ### Alphabetical Register of Heretics

89

One should not wonder at this diversity <note>Referring to the conflicting reports from different historians regarding the Council’s proceedings.</note>, as there are few places in *Church History* where one can observe more confusion and negligence than in the history of this famous *Council*. One has also been obliged to draw what will be said about it from various historical writers, because none of the ancients gave a complete account of it. With respect to the diversity noted among historians regarding the same matter, the oldest sources have been followed here, or those with the greatest probability. *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), often called the "Father of Church History."</note>, who was present at this *Council*, only touched upon the circumstances of this history in passing, possibly out of fear of offending the *Arians* or the *Orthodox*. Since then, this matter has never been spoken of with complete neutrality, and rumors have often been recounted as truth.

*Eusebius* strongly praises these *Bishops* who were present there, but *Sabinus* (*Socrates, Book 8*) <note>original: "Socrat. lib. 8." refers to the historian Socrates Scholasticus.</note>, a *Macedonian Bishop* <note>A follower of Macedonius I of Constantinople; this group, also known as the Pneumatomachi, held specific views regarding the Holy Spirit that differed from the Nicene Creed.</note> of *Heraclea*, a city of *Thrace*, called them ignorant in his collection of the *Councils*; there was also a great number of Priests and Deacons who had come with the *Bishops*.

The *Council* began on June 14, and various matters were determined there which we will not dwell upon here, but only touch upon what happened there regarding the primary dispute that was decided, namely *Arianism*. As

As soon as the bishops had arrived, they held private meetings incessantly and called *Arius* (*Sozomen, Book 1, Chapters 17 & 19*) <note>Sozomen (c. 400–450 AD) was a historian of the early Church; his "Ecclesiastical History" is a major source for this period.</note> into their midst to learn of his opinions. After they had heard his thoughts from him, some judged that all innovations should be condemned, and that one should be satisfied to speak of the Son using the same expressions that their ancestors had used; and the others thought that the opinions of the ancients should not be accepted without investigation. There were 17 bishops, according to the account of *Sozomen, Book 1, Chapter 20*, who favored the new interpretations of *Arius*, the most prominent of whom were *Eusebius of Nicomedia*, *Eusebius of Caesarea*, *Menophantes of Ephesus*, *Patrophilus of Scythopolis*, *Theognis of Nicaea*, *Narcissus of Neronias*, *Theonas of Marmarica*, and *Secundus of Ptolemais*. These bishops drew up a *Confession of Faith* (*Theodoret, Book 1, Chapter 7, from Athanasius*) <note>original: "Theod. lib. 1. cap. 7. ex Athanasio"; refers to Theodoret of Cyrus, a 5th-century bishop and historian.</note> according to their views, but they had no sooner read it in the *Assembly* than it was decried as false; it was torn to pieces, and they were reviled as men who wished to betray the *Faith*. A letter from *Eusebius of Nicomedia*, in which he expressed his thoughts, suffered the same fate.

They then worked to design a *Symbol* or short *Statement of Faith* <note>original: "Symbolus"; in this context, it refers to the formulation of a creed or summary of essential Christian beliefs.</note> in which the views set against those of *Arius* would be established. It was immediately noted that the new ways of speaking, which the *Arians* used,

### Alphabetical List of Heretics: AR

->91<-

<note>original: "AR der Ketteren"; abbreviation for Arius or Arianism.</note>

[The council] had to condemn the notion that the *Son* was brought forth out of nothing, that he was a creature, that there had been a time when he did not exist, etc., and instead make use of the idioms of Scripture, such as these: that the *Son is unique by his nature*, that he is the *Word*, the *Power*, and the *Wisdom* of his *Father*, the *reflection of his glory*, and the *exact image of his substance* <note>original: "zelfstandigheid"; a translation of the Greek *hypostasis* or Latin *substantia*, referring to the fundamental nature of God.</note>. The *Arians* having testified that they were ready to accept a *Confession of Faith* contained in these expressions, the orthodox *Bishops* feared that they might interpret these words in a bad sense. Therefore, they wanted to add that the *Son is of the substance of the Father*, because this is what distinguishes the *Son* from the creatures.

Regarding this, the *Arians* were asked whether they did not agree that the *Son* is no creature, but the *Power*, the *only Wisdom*, and the *Image of the Father*; that he is *eternal*, and in *all things equal* to the *Father*, and finally *truly God*. The unorthodox, having spoken with one another, thought that these expressions could very well correspond with the idea they had of the divinity of the Son, and indicated that they were ready to accept them. 

Finally, since it had been noted that *Eusebius of Nicomedia* <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia was the primary political supporter of Arius and a key figure in the early Arian controversy.</note> in the letter that had been read rejected the expression *consubstantial* <note>original: "medezelfstandig"; this is the Dutch translation for the Greek term *homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance." It became the defining word of the Nicene Creed.</note>, it was thought that the orthodox doctrine could not be better expressed, nor all ambiguity excluded, than by making use of that term—all the more so because the *Arians* testified that they feared it. # AR of the Heretics.

"That day having come, the Emperor took all their *Petitions*, had the same cast into the fire, and commanded them to forgive one another reciprocally, according to the precepts of the *Gospel*." <note>Constantine burned the letters of accusation to ensure the bishops focused on church unity rather than personal grievances.</note> He subsequently commanded them to labor toward the clarification of the *Articles of Faith*, upon which they were to judge, and established a fixed day on which a decision would be made regarding the dispute of *Consubstantiality*. <note>original: "Medezelfstandigheid"; a Dutch rendering of the Greek term *homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance," which was the central theological debate regarding the nature of Jesus Christ.</note>

The appointed day having come, *Eusebius* (*The Life of Constantine, book 3, chapter 10*) <note>Eusebius of Caesarea was a historian and bishop who was a primary chronicler of Constantine’s reign.</note> relates that *Constantine* had all the Bishops assemble in a hall of his Palace, where he had caused chairs to be placed on the right and left sides. The Bishops first entered therein, and subsequently the Emperor arrived; he did not seat himself at the head of the Assembly in a golden Throne, which he had placed there, until after the Bishops had given him a sign to sit down. Being seated, *Eusebius of Caesarea*, who was at his right side, delivered an address to him, thanking him for the care he took to preserve the purity of the faith in the *Christian Church*. *Constantine* then took the floor and delivered an *Oration* in *Latin*, <note>original: "Redenvoering in ’t Latyn"</note> in which he testified that nothing grieved him so much as the division he perceived among the *Christians*, and powerfully exhorted the Bishops to peace. An *Interpreter* <note>original: "Tolk"</note> thereafter translated this *Oration* into *Greek*, because the *Eastern Bishops* did not understand *Latin*.

...had prepared in the private meetings that had been held beforehand, there nevertheless immediately arose a great *Debate*, and *Constantine* had the patience to listen to the long-lasting disputes, in which he acted as an arbiter, attempting to bring those into agreement whose opinions or expressions seemed too extreme; and supporting the reasons that he thought were well-founded; also praising those who he believed spoke with fairness. *Eusebius* of *Caesarea* for a long time resisted (*Socrates, book 1, chapter 8, & Theodoret, book 1, chapter 12*) <note>Latin citations: "Socrat. lib. 1. cap. 8. & Theod. lib. 1. cap. 12." He proposed another *Creed*, in which the same term was omitted, and in which he called the Son *solely God born of God, light come forth from light, life issued from life, only Son, firstborn of all creatures, generated from his Father before all ages.* The Emperor approved this confession and exhorted the Fathers of the Synod to follow the same, only adding the word *Consubstantial* thereto.

Subsequently, the *Creed* was read, which had been drawn up with this word, the expressions of which have already been recounted. *Anathemas* <note>original: "Anathemaas"; formal ecclesiastical curses or excommunications pronounced against those who hold heretical beliefs.</note> were added thereto, against those who on this occasion would make use of expressions other than those of *Holy Scripture*; which must be understood in such a way that those were exempted which the Council should find good and sanctify. In particular, this proposition was condemned: that the *Son did not exist before he was ge-* <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword "ge-" likely refers to the word "gegenereert" (generated), completing the Arian phrase "There was a time when the Son was not," which the Council condemned.</note>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Council of Nicaea (325 AD) and the specific language used in the Nicene Creed to oppose Arianism. It follows the previous discussion of Eusebius of Caesarea's initial hesitation to accept the term "Consubstantial."</meta>

# ...of the Heretics.

...had been begotten; *Eusebius* and others asserted that the expressions of the *Creed* <note>original: "Symbolum"; the formal statement of Christian belief</note> and of the *Anathemas* <note>original: "Anathemaas"; formal curses or excommunications against heretical views</note> should be explained. 

1. It is said that the word *Begotten* <note>original: "Gegenereert"</note> was used, and not that of *Made*, because this latter word signifies the production of creatures, to which the *Son* has no likeness, being of a much more glorious *Substance* <note>original: "Zelfstandigheid"; in this context, it refers to the Greek "ousia" or the essential nature of God</note> than they, begotten by the *Father* in an incomprehensible manner. 

2. The word *Consubstantial* <note>original: "Medezelfstandig"; the Dutch equivalent of the Greek "homoousios"</note> is proper to the *Son*; not in the sense in which it is used when one speaks of bodies, or of mortal animals; the *Son* not being *consubstantial* with the *Father* through a division of the *Divine Substance*, of which he would possess a part, nor through a change of that same *Substance*. By this, one means nothing else than that the *Son* has no similarity to the creatures he brought forth, but that he is equal in all things to his *Father*, by whom he was begotten, or that he is of no other existence or substance, but of that of his *Father*.

3. They condemned those who said that the *Son* did not exist before he was born, since he existed before his physical birth, and even before his *Divine* generation, according to the reasoning of *Eusebius*. ### Alphabetical Name List AR

...being, as he is always King and Savior, and all-powerful, being eternally in the same state.

This will perhaps seem like pure *Arianism* <note>original: "Arianerey"; the belief that Jesus Christ was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father</note>, and a denial of the eternity of the **Son**; but one must note that, in the style of that time, to exist before the world and to be eternal are the same thing; because to prove his eternity one brings up this passage, *In the beginning was the Word*, and one is satisfied to show that he was begotten <note>original: "gegenereert"</note> before there was any time. Thus one must not reject these words as fabricated solely for this reason; and it is so common to find harsh expressions among those who have tried in some way to express this incomprehensible mystery, that if one were to judge them by that, one would have to declare them all *Heretics*, that is to say, pronounce the *Anathema* <note>original: "Anathema"; a formal curse or decree of excommunication used by the Church</note> over the greater part of the ancients. Besides this, **St. Athanasius**, who openly considers **Eusebius** an *Arian* (*On the Decrees of Nicaea <note>original: "De decret. Niceen."</note>, Vol. 1, page 251*), alludes to a part of this passage and draws a conclusion from it that **Eusebius** would undoubtedly never have acknowledged: that the *Arians* believed the Divinity of **Jesus Christ** did not exist before his bodily birth.

After these explanations, **Eusebius** signed—as he himself testifies in the letter that has been produced—although he had refused to do so the previous day. The long and powerful opposition which he had made against the word <term>Consubstantial</term> <note>original: "Mede zelfftandig"; the Dutch translation of the Greek "homoousios," meaning "of the same substance"</note>...

Subsequently, **Arius** and those of his faction were excommunicated <note>original: "in den ban gedaan"; placed under a formal ecclesiastical curse or ban</note>, and all their books were condemned, particularly a poetic work that **Arius** had named *Thalia* <note>original: "Thalia"; a work by Arius designed to spread his theological views through popular song</note>. After this, the Council wrote a circular letter to the Churches of **Egypt**, in which it made known how the matter of **Arius** had transpired, and what had been deemed appropriate regarding **Meletius** <note>original: "Melecius"; referring to Meletius of Lycopolis, a bishop whose dissent caused a schism in the Egyptian church</note>, a schismatic bishop, and the observance of the **Easter festival** <note>original: "Paaschfeest"</note>. **Constantine** also wrote to the Church of **Alexandria** to assure them that after a careful investigation, **Arius** had been condemned with universal consent. Through another letter addressed to the Bishops and to the Churches, he commands that **Arius** be given the name of **Porphyrius**, and to call his followers **Porphyrians**. This **Porphyry** was a renowned **Platonist**, who had written against the **Christian Religion**, and whose books **Constantine** had ordered to be burned. **Lucas Holstenius** <note>A 17th-century German humanist and geographer who served as the librarian of the Vatican.</note> has described his life, which can be found at the end of the book *On Abstinence from Animal Food* <note>original: "van de onthouding der dieren"; referring to Porphyry's work *De Abstinentia ab Esu Animalium*</note>. **Constantine** intended by this to declare **Arius** an enemy of the **Christian Religion**, and by no means to reproach him for being a **Platonist** regarding the **Trinity**; because **Constantine**, as has been seen, did not judge the sentiments of **Plato** unfavorably.

It is true that one attributes to the **Arians** an all too great attachment to the reading of this **Philosopher** and to the **Pagan Writers**. **Constantine** also commanded in that same letter that all the **Books of Arius** be burned, so that not only his evil teachings were destroyed, but that no memory of them remained for posterity. He also declared that if anyone hid any of his **Books**, and did not have them burned, he would be punished with death immediately after he was convicted of it. There is another letter from this Emperor, in which he commands all the Churches to celebrate the **Easter festival** according to the **Canons of the Council** <note>The Council of Nicaea established a uniform date for Easter, separate from the Jewish Passover.</note>.

### AR of the Heretics.

Those who signed the Council were *Hosius*, Bishop of *Cordua* <note>modern-day Córdoba, Spain; he was a close advisor to Emperor Constantine</note>; followed by *Viton* and *Vincent*, Roman Priests sent by *Sylvester* <note>Pope Sylvester I, who did not attend the council in person due to old age</note>, after them the Bishops of *Alexandria*, of *Antioch*, and of *Jerusalem*, and finally the other Bishops. Those who favored the claims of the Roman Court say that *Hosius* signed as a <term>Legate</term> <note>an official representative or envoy, in this case representing the Pope's authority</note> of the Bishop of that City [Rome], but the oldest historians do not say so.

The Council having come to an end on the 25th of *August*, *Augustinus* <note>The text here names "Augustinus" (Augustine), though historical records of the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD typically credit the closing speech to Emperor Constantine the Great. St. Augustine of Hippo was not born until 354 AD, making this likely a historical error or a reference to a different tradition by the author.</note> took his leave of the assembly and delivered a beautiful oration, in which he exhorted the *Fathers* <note>The gathered bishops</note> to peace and to bear with one another mutually; but this was fruitless, as shall appear in the sequel. Thus ended this famous *Council*, the circumstances of which would be better known to us if the fear of offending powerful persons, the zeal of some, the passion of others, and the reverence which posterity has held for the determinations and decrees of such a famous assembly had not prevented the writers of that time from describing its history with the accuracy and impartiality observed in true historians, and restrained those who have lived since that time from saying what they perhaps knew of it that was unfavorable. *Athanasius*, in a small treatise which has already been cited, and in which at first glance he seems to want to describe its history, being carried away by the zeal with—

The unorthodox <note>original: "onregtzinnigen"; here referring to the Arian or Meletian factions who disagreed with the Nicene Council</note> claim on the contrary that the *Meletians* <note>a schismatic group in Egypt founded by Meletius of Lycopolis</note>, having reunited themselves with the orthodox after the death of *Alexander*, 54 Bishops of *Egypt* bound themselves by oath to choose his successor with common consent; but that seven of them broke their oath and chose *Athanasius* without the knowledge of the others. Some even assert that the votes being divided and the election not happening quickly enough, *Athanasius* shut himself in with two Bishops in the Church of *St. Denys* <note>St. Dionysius</note> and had himself consecrated there against the will of the other Bishops, who indeed had the church doors broken open, but too late, the ceremony having already been completed. Alphabetische Naamlyst AR

There may well be much passion on the side of the heterodox <note>original: "onregtzinnigen"; those holding opinions at odds with the established Church</note>, but since people can become as heated for the truth as for a lie, and since the good cause is sometimes advocated through bad means, it would not be certain to reject everything the heterodox say, nor to blindly accept everything the orthodox relate about it.

It seems that *Constantine* at that time made his decree (*Eusebius in his Life [of Constantine], book 3, chapter 64*) against the meetings of all Heretics, in which he forbids them to meet in public or in private, gives their Chapels to the orthodox, and declares the Houses, in which they might be found gathered, performing their Religious ceremonies, to be forfeited. *Eusebius* adds to this that the *Edict* <note>an official order or proclamation issued by a person in authority</note> of the Emperor also stipulated that one should seize all the Books of the heretics; and that the threats of *Constantinus* forced a great number of Heretics and *Schismatics* <note>original: "Scheurmakers"; those who promote division or a split within a religious body</note> to join the orthodox Church. But as some did so sincerely, and others through force, the Bishops applied themselves earnestly to distinguish between them, and received only those into the Church who had truly converted. The *Arians* <note>followers of the teachings of Arius regarding the nature of the Trinity</note> were thoroughly suppressed by special *Edicts*, so that nearly all the heresies in the *Roman Empire* seemed to have been destroyed.

->### AR of the Heretics.<-

But ***Constantine***, who at the start had disregarded the cause of the dispute between ***Arius*** and ***Alexander*** <note>Alexander of Alexandria, the bishop who first opposed Arius</note> as consisting only of different expressions, and who had subsequently regarded it as a matter of the utmost importance, finally viewed ***Arius*** as orthodox <note>original: "regtzinnig"; holding the established, "correct" religious beliefs</note> once more. This was perhaps because he acted according to his current interests, or because he allowed himself to be guided by those who were most intimate with him, or finally, because he truly changed his opinions. (***Socrates [Scholasticus], Book 1, Chapter 25, from Rufinus***)

***Constantia***, sister of ***Constantine*** and widow of ***Licinius***, had among her household a Priest who was a friend of ***Arius*** and shared his views. This priest persuaded the Princess that ***Arius*** did not hold the views attributed to him—at least not in the way they were usually expressed—and that ***Alexander*** had accused him out of envy because he was held in high regard by the people, and that the Council <note>the Council of Nicaea, 325 AD</note> had treated him unfairly. 

***Constantia***, who placed great trust in this clergyman, believed him easily, but she did not dare to speak of it to the Emperor. Having fallen dangerously ill, all she could do before her death was to recommend this Priest to her brother as an exceptionally virtuous man who was most faithful in the service of the Imperial House. She died shortly thereafter, and this Priest, having gained the respect of ***Constantine***, spoke with him at a certain time on the same subject. He said that if the Emperor wished for ***Arius*** to appear before him and [explain] his thoughts...

After this Priest had assured him of this, ***Constantine*** had ***Arius*** notified that he should come to Court; and since ***Arius*** had not dared to do so immediately, the Emperor wrote him a letter by which he commanded him to come as quickly as possible and to make use of the <term>public carriage</term> <note>original: "openbare Rytuig"; referring to the *cursus publicus*, the state-run transport system of the Roman Empire, a privilege usually reserved for officials</note>. ***Arius*** obeyed this repeated command, and having arrived at *Constantinople* with ***Euzoius*** <note>a deacon and close ally of Arius</note>, they offered the Emperor a *Confession of Faith*, in which they simply said that they believed that the *Son* was born of the *Vader* before all ages, and that the *Word* <note>the theological concept of the *Logos*</note>, which is *God*, had made all things both in heaven and on earth. If ***Constantine*** was truly satisfied with this declaration, he must have changed his opinion, or paid little attention to it, or understood the *Nicene Creed* very little. Whatever the case may be, it appeared from the outcome that the *Arian Bishops* gradually returned to favor, and that the Emperor treated ***Arius*** with much gentleness and allowed him to return to *Alexandria*. 

One does not know the exact time when ***Arius*** was recalled, but it is known that this occurred before the recall of ***Eusebius*** and ***Theognis*** <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia and Theognis of Nicaea, prominent supporters of Arius</note>, which took place three years...

These two bishops <note>referring to Eusebius of Nicomedia and Theognis of Nicaea, who had been exiled for their support of Arius</note> wrote a letter from the place of their exile (*Socrates Scholasticus, Book 1, Chapter 14*), in which they complained:

"That they had been condemned without being heard, although [the Council] had been satisfied with their conduct at the *Council of Nicaea*, where, having carefully considered the word *Consubstantial* <note>original: "Medezelfstandig"; the Dutch equivalent of the Greek *Homoousios*, the controversial term used to define the Son as being of the same substance as the Father</note>, they had finally approved it. They added that they had only refused to condemn *Arius* because they knew that he was not as he was described; and that, since this had been recognized by recalling him from exile, it would not be fair that they, who suffered for his sake, remained banished after his recall."

This letter was addressed to the principal bishops, whom they begged to speak for them to the Emperor. Speaking of the recall of *Arius*, they attributed it to these bishops: *Your compassion*, they said, *has seen fit to treat him gently and to recall him.* A learned man (*Valesius* <note>Henri Valois, a 17th-century French scholar and editor of ancient church histories</note> *in his commentary on this passage*) remarks on this place that *Eusebius* and *Theognis* attribute to the bishops that which the Emperor had done, since it was he who had recalled *Arius*; and that ecclesiastical historians also sometimes attribute to the Emperor the actions of the bishops. For example, when *Socrates* <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note> says that the *Council of Nicaea* forbade *Arius* from entering *Alexandria* again, whereas it was actually the Emperor who did so. But the truth is that the Emperor at that time little <note>The sentence continues on the next page: "...did little [without the advice of the bishops]."</note>

<summary>This page describes the efforts of the exiled Arian bishops Eusebius and Theognis to be reinstated by arguing that their only crime was defending Arius, who had since been pardoned by the Emperor.</summary>

The letter from *Eusebius* and *Theognis* <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia and Theognis of Nicaea, leaders of the Arian faction</note> had the effect they had hoped for; they were recalled along with *Theonas* and *Secundus*, who had refused to sign anything <note>Theonas of Marmarica and Secundus of Ptolemais were the only bishops who had flatly refused to sign the Nicene Creed alongside Arius</note>. Having returned to their dioceses, the first two drove out those who had been placed there in their stead when they were sent into exile. Immediately afterward, they were accused of seeking ways to make *Athanasius* <note>St. Athanasius the Great, the staunch defender of Nicene orthodoxy and Bishop of Alexandria</note> suffer the same punishment they had endured, by telling the Emperor that his election had not occurred in a *canonical* <note>original: "Canonyke"; meaning according to the established laws or "canons" of the Church</note> manner; and they had tried, through entreaties and threats, to persuade the same *Athanasius* to allow *Arius* to return to *Alexandria*. However, they could not achieve their objective at that time, and we shall see hereafter what difficulties they had with that bishop.

After the *Council of Nicaea* had adjourned and they had been banished, these actions and the decrees of the Council had only superficially settled the disputes, which persisted even after they were recalled. *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the church historian</note> asserts that the bishops of *Egypt* had been in great disagreement since then, and *Socrates* <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note> says, *Book 1, Chapter 23* <note>original: "lib. 1. cap. 23."</note>, that he perceived through the letters of the bishops of those times that some were very offended by the word <term>consubstantial</term> <note>original: "medezelfstandig"; the Dutch equivalent of the Greek *Homoousios*, the term defining the Son as being of the same essence as the Father</note>. He also accused **Origen** of having explained the **Holy Scripture** according to the ideas of the **Pagan Philosophers**, and particularly according to those of **Plato**, from whom **Marcellus** claimed that he [Origen] had derived his doctrine of the **Primary Causes**—that is, of the **Holy Trinity**—of which he had spoken in the **Platonic manner**. ### Alphabetical List of Heretics: AR

...in a church called Peace <note>Hagia Irene (Holy Peace) in Constantinople</note>, and that he set himself to pray fervently to God—not that he might convert *Arius*, or even make him confess the truth; but that if *Arius’s* opinion were true, he [the Bishop] might not even see the day set for speaking of it: or that if his own faith were true, *Arius*, who was the cause of so many calamities, might be punished for his treachery. A prayer with so little love, and through which one could see that this Bishop was more concerned for his honor than for the truth, did not lack for an answer: for on the following day, which was a Sunday, or on the evening of that same day, as *Arius* was going to the church, accompanied by those of his faction, or to another place—for the historians differ—and coming across the Forum of *Constantine*, he felt such a great urge to attend to his necessities that he was forced to go into the public latrines or privies. There, instead of finding relief, his bowels emptied out, and thus he died suddenly. After that time, these latrines were shown to passers-by, and no one dared to sit in the place where *Arius* had sat. It is said that a wealthy *Arian* <note>A follower of Arius's teachings</note>, in order to destroy the memory of it, later bought this place from the public and had a house built there.

Thus *Rufinus*, *Socrates*, and *Sozomen* <note>These are the names of prominent early Christian historians: Rufinus of Aquileia, Socrates Scholasticus, and Sozomen</note> recount the end of *Arius’s* life. ...the detention of those of his faction having been revoked, he offered his confession of faith to the Emperor, and swore that he believed nothing else [than the orthodox faith]; after which those who protected him, coming from the Imperial Palace, intended to lead him into the Church. A learned man, *M. de Valois* <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), also known as Valesius, was a renowned French scholar who edited the works of early church historians</note>, has therefore argued that the *Arius* who was received at *Jerusalem*—and who was a priest belonging to the faction of the famous *Arius*—was not the same man who died outside the communion of the Church. He suggests this because, otherwise, one would have to admit that *Athanasius* <note>The Bishop of Alexandria and the most fierce opponent of Arianism</note> was mistaken in his account. 

But even if one allowed him the point that this Bishop [Athanasius] had erred while speaking of a man whom he insulted at every opportunity, one should not find it strange; especially since he was not present in *Constantinople* when the events he recounts took place. One could also say that *Athanasius* recounts briefly, and without strict precision, what he had heard said of *Arius*. He may have regarded him as having remained under the ban <term>excommunication</term> because he had only been readmitted by a Council whose authority *Athanasius* did not recognize—a council consisting primarily of persons whose opinions had been condemned and rejected at *Nicaea* <note>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD, which first condemned Arianism as heresy</note>. It is much more natural to interpret this passage of *Athanasius* in this manner than to declare as false a history as detailed as that of the final years of the life of *Arius*, regarding...

...certain cases, which the historians cited had no reason to alter. This death of *Arius* is said to have occurred in the year 336. However, renowned scholars reject this chronology. *Henricus Valesius* <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French historian of the Church known for his critical editions of ancient ecclesiastical texts</note> demonstrates that *Arius* was not alive at the time of the Synod of *Jerusalem*, which received the letters of *Constantine* regarding the acceptance of some prominent members of the *Arian faction* <term>Arianerey: the movement following Arius, which claimed that Jesus was a created being and not of the same substance as God the Father</term>. Thus, it was not *Arius* the Arch-heretic <note>original: "Aartsketter"</note> who was presented to this Council by *Constantine* and who found the Bishops gathered at *Jerusalem* so favorable. *Socrates* <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian of the early Church</note> nevertheless says clearly that the Council of *Tyre*—which was moved to *Jerusalem* for the dedication of the Temple—received *Arius* and his followers into the communion of the Church by virtue of the letters of *Constantine*, which testified that he was convinced of the orthodoxy of *Arius* and of that of *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia, the influential bishop who served as the primary political protector of the Arian movement</note>.

*Constantine* had sent to the Bishops gathered at *Jerusalem* the confession of faith which *Arius* and *Euzoïus* <note>A deacon and close companion of Arius</note> had offered him, and *Athanasius* says explicitly that the Synod of *Jerusalem* received *Arius* and his supporters into communion. *Valesius* removes this difficulty by saying that there were two persons of the name *Arius*: one was the Arch-heretic, and the other was a follower of the Arch-heretic. They had both been excommunicated <term>in den ban gedaan: officially excluded from the sacraments and membership of the Church</term> by *Alexander*, Bishop of *Alexandria*. The one who, along with *Euzoïus*, offered a confession of faith and was accepted by the Synod of *Jerusalem*, was the...

...was not the **Arch-heretic** <note>original: "Aartsketter"</note>; it was the other *Arius*. *Valesius* <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French historian and philologist specializing in early church history</note> proves this not only through the arguments he presents to show that the **Arch-heretic** had died long before the year 335, but also through the *petition*, or the Letter of *Eusebius* and *Theognis* <note>Theognis of Nicaea, an early 4th-century bishop and supporter of Arius</note>. These two Bishops requested mercy, testifying to their innocence in the year 328, and they asserted that the head and the cause of these disputes had been accepted and restored. One could not say this of this *Arius*, who was reunited with the Church at the Synod of *Jerusalem*, because the *petition* or the *confession of faith* offered by him and *Euzoius* <note>A follower of Arius and later Bishop of Antioch</note> to the Emperor testifies that he was still in exile and under the ban <term>ban: excommunication or formal exclusion from the church community</term>, this being done by him a little before this Synod—that is to say, around the year 335. This sudden death of *Arius*, in which the orthodox have found so many mysteries <note>The "mysteries" refer to the traditional belief that Arius’s sudden, violent death in a public latrine was a sign of divine judgment</note>, occurred after the Council of *Jerusalem*. This *Arius*, who died in the aforementioned manner, must then not have been the **Arch-heretic**, or one must have transposed to one time that which happened in another. It is strange that so little order and accuracy is found in Church History: one could not confirm the exile of *Arius*, its duration, and similar matters, except by reasoning through certain instances, some of which are testified to by one party and some by others. A single good historian, if the others were to be lost, would be enough to...

*Eusebius, Bishop of Nicomedia*, expecting everything from *Constantius* <note>Constantius II, the son of Constantine the Great, who was much more sympathetic to the Arian cause than his father had been</note> after the death of the great *Constantine*, openly labored, along with *Theognis, Bishop of Nicaea*, to destroy the faith of the *Council of Nicaea* and to remove the expression *Consubstantial* <term>consubstantial: a translation of the Greek term *Homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance," used by the Nicene Creed to describe the relationship between God the Father and God the Son; the Dutch original is "Medezelfstandig"</term>. About ninety *Bishops* were present there, among whom were thirty-six from the faction of *Eusebius*. The orthodox, who were greater in number but then without power, were forced to appear in this assembly as well. There they examined the case of *Athanasius, Patriarch of Alexandria*, even though the *Pope* had already gathered a *Council* in *Rome* to judge that matter. *Eusebius*, who had total direction over this unlawful *Church Assembly* <note>often referred to as the "Council of the Dedication"</note>, had *Athanasius* deposed once again, whether because the orthodox *Bishops* remained silent on this occasion or because their opposition was not counted due to the authority of the Emperor *Constantius*, who was present at this condemnation. ...which nevertheless left freedom to always maintain *Arianism*. Their first profession of faith was: *That they believed in one single God, Creator of all, and in his only Son, who was with his Father before all ages, and through whom all things were made, and who shall be God and King throughout all the ages.* But because they rightly thought that this profession of faith would undoubtedly make them suspect <note>The Arians were suspected of using vague language to hide their belief that Jesus was a created being rather than eternally divine</note>, they drew up another one a few days later which stated that they believed the *Word of God*, according to the *Gospel*, which says, *and the Word was God*, and that he is *unchangeable, the Image of the Divinity, of the Essence, and of the glory of his Father*, without any distinction: and finally that they recognized the *Father*, the *Son*, and the *Holy Spirit* as three distinct *Persons*, and who are but one in *sentiment*, and in the *will*. This is orthodox when it is well understood according to the sense of the Scripture; but not by [omitting] saying that the *Son* is of the same *substance* <term>substance: the Dutch original is "zelfstandigheid," referring to the Greek "ousia" or "hypostasis," the underlying reality of God’s being</term> with his *Father*; and that the three persons, who are but one in *Will*, are also one single *essence*; thus they always had the opportunity to give an *Arian* meaning to their words. They nevertheless regretted that they had said so much by professing that the *Son* was the *Image of the substance, and of the Divinity of his Father, without any distinction* <note>original Greek: "ἀπαράλλακτον εἰκόνα" (aparallakton eikona), meaning an "unchanging image" or "exact likeness"</note>. This caused them to be called *Semi-Arians* <note>original: "halve Arianen"</note>, because they retained the foundations of *Arius* but rejected the consequences thereof. To give authority to this unlawful assembly, they had the audacity to call it the *Holy Council of Sardica*. This has led those into error who make no distinction between the orthodox *Council of Sardica* and the *Arian* one of *Philippopolis*.

The Emperor *Constans*, an enemy of *Arianism* <note>original: "Arianerey"</note>, compelled the Emperor *Constantius* his brother to consent to the restoration of *Athanasius*, who returned to his Church in *Alexandria*: but after the death of *Constans* in the year 350, the faction of the *Arians* began to strengthen itself again against the Orthodox, whom *Constantius* cruelly persecuted. *Acacius of Caesarea*, who had succeeded *Eusebius*, and who had not wished to reconcile with the *Semi-Arians*, but had become the head of those who openly and without any mitigation [taught] *Arianism*...

AR of the Heretics. 131

Confession of Faith <note>original: "Geloofs-Formulier"</note>, which had been drawn up six years earlier at the illegal Church assembly of *Sirmium* <note>a major city in the Roman province of Pannonia, modern-day Serbia</note>, against *Photinus*, because it came very close to the orthodox doctrine—with the exception of the word *consubstantial* <note>original: "Medezelfstandig," referring to the belief that the Father and Son are of the very same substance</note>—these *Arians*, it is said, held a meeting on their own authority and drew up an eighth formula. In this, they rejected the two expressions of *consubstantial*, or *substantial*, and of *similar in substance*, or *co-essential*, <term>of the same substance <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος"</note></term> and <term>of similar substance <note>original: "ὁμοιούσιος"</note></term>, under the pretext that these are not found in the Scriptures. They declared that the *Father* was greater than the *Son* in dignity, in honor, in radiance, and in *Majesty*, and that the *Son* was subject to him. It was not difficult for them to have this approved by the Emperor, of whose mind they were masters, in the absence of the Bishops who were not pure *Arians* but had somewhat faltered in that opinion; at their instigation, the Emperor even made *Hosius* <note>original: "Osius"</note> sign the *Arian Formula* and forced Pope *Liberius*, during his exile, to consent to the abolition of the word *Consubstantial*.

In the year 358, an illegal *Arian Church Council* was held in *Ancyra* <note>modern-day Ankara, Turkey</note>, the capital of *Galatia*. that war was declared among the *Arians*, who since then were divided into two camps. Before the *Council of Nicaea* <note>The landmark 325 AD council that defined the relationship between God the Father and the Son</note>, they were all pure *Arians*. After that *Council*, during the life of *Constantine*, they feigned orthodoxy out of fear of exile; and after the death of this great Prince, they were nearly all *Semi-Arians*, being content to deny the *Consubstantiality of the Word* <note>original: "Medezelfstandigheid van het Woord"; the belief that the Father and Son are of the same essence</note>, and condemning the other hateful propositions of *Arius*. 

But after the assembly of *Ancyra* <note>held in 358 AD in modern-day Turkey</note>, they divided into pure *Arians* or *Anomoeans* <note>from the Greek "anomoios," meaning "unlike," referring to the belief that the Son is unlike the Father</note> and *Semi-Arians*, who mutually condemned one another in their Councils. The strict *Arians* followed the heresy of *Arius* exactly as it was at its birth; and their principal leaders were then *Eudoxius*, Patriarch of *Antioch*, protector of *Aëtius*, *Acacius*, Bishop of *Caesarea*, *Valens of Mursa*, *Ursacius of Singidunum*, and several others. 

The *Semi-Arians* said that the Son of God was similar in substance with his *Father*, <term>of similar substance <note>original Greek: "ὁμοιούσιος" (homoiousios)</note></term>, and they had as their principal leaders *Basil*, Bishop of *Ancyra*, *George of Laodicea*, *Eustathius of Sebaste*, and various others; of whom some held that the *Word* had begun to exist, but before all ages; and others, that the same had existed from all eternity, although they stubbornly maintained, like all the other *Arians*, that he was not of the same substance as his *Father*. The Emperor, in the same year, had *Pope Liberius* come to *Sirmium*, where he, in the presence of all the Bishop-

### AR of the Heretics.

I 3

...bishops, who were at the Court, pressed him to openly declare that he confessed that the *Son of God* was not <term>consubstantial <note>original Dutch: "medezelffstandig"; equivalent to the Greek "homoousios," meaning of the same essence</note></term> with his *Father*. *Liberius* <note>Pope Liberius, who reigned from 352–366 AD; his temporary yielding to Arian pressure remains a point of historical debate</note> refused to do so, whereupon *Basil of Ancyra* proposed a collection, containing the decrees against *Paul of Samosata*, which the entire Church had accepted, the *Formulary* <note>A formal statement of belief or creed</note> of the *Council* of the dedication at *Antioch*, and that of *Sirmium* against *Photinus*, in which there was nothing that offended the orthodox doctrine, except for the removal of the word *consubstantial*, which all the *Arians* rejected. Then *Liberius* signed, in order to escape exile, and returned to *Rome*, where he nobly defended the faith of the *Council of Nicaea*. In the year 359, a *Council* was assembled at *Seleucia*, and at the same time another at *Rimini*. The leaders of the *Arians*, judging that they would better achieve their goal if the *Easterners* were separated from the *Westerners*; obtained from the Emperor *Constantius*, who was then at *Sirmium*, that the *Bishops* be divided into two *Councils*, and that those of the *East* should gather at *Seleucia*, while those of the *West* held their *Council* at *Rimini*, a city in *Italy*. But the Emperor commanded them at the same time to jointly draft a *Confession of Faith*, in order to present it to both the *Councils*. They created one, which entailed that when speaking of God and of his *Son*, they would abolish the expression of *substance* <note>original: "zelffstandigheid"</note>, and that it was true that the *Son* is like his *Father*, in all things.

(a) *Catacombs are passages under the earth in Italy, but especially in and around Rome, the actual origin of which has not yet been discovered, although one can conclude from various epitaphs found therein that some of the first Christians are buried there. Similar ones are also found near the City of Naples.*

At last, after so many *Arian Councils* had been held, in which several *Creeds* were made in less than twenty years—starting from the first, which was drawn up in the year 341, at the *Dedication of Antioch*—he finally allowed the most abominable of all to be made public.

After his death and under the Empire of *Julian the Apostate*, *Athanasius* <note>St. Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD), the chief defender of Trinitarian orthodoxy against Arianism.</note> held a Council at *Alexandria*, in which it was decided that all the Bishops who might have been in communion with the *Arians* through weakness or by surprise should be restored to their dignity, if they confessed the Faith of *Nicaea*. There they also established the *Divinity* of the *Holy Spirit*, which the *Semi-Arians* began to deny. They also condemned their *Creed of Sardica*, and *Athanasius* united there the *Latins* and the *Greeks* over the expression of *subsistence* <note>original: "bestaanlykheid"; referring to the Greek term 'hypostasis'.</note>, which the former took for the *Person*, and the others for the *Substance* <note>original: "Zelfftandigheid"; referring to 'ousia'.</note>. He showed them that they all said the same thing, using the same word, to which they gave two different meanings: for the *Greeks* held that there were three *subsistences* in God...

The Emperor *Valens* meanwhile cruelly persecuted the Orthodox in the East, and caused the *Goths* to embrace *Arianism* <note>The conversion of the Goths to Arian Christianity under Valens meant that many Germanic tribes would remain Arians long after the Roman Empire returned to Nicene orthodoxy.</note>. This chapel later became a large and magnificent temple through the generosity of the Emperors. to embrace <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page, describing the religious mandate to accept the Nicene Creed.</note>, (by which one confessed a single *Essence* <note>original: "Wezen"; referring to the theological concept of *ousia* or substance.</note> and a single *Deity* in the *trinity* of *Persons*, of the *Father*, of the *Son*, and of the *Holy Spirit*) under the threat of being considered *public Heretics*. He subsequently caused all the churches in *Constantinople* to be placed back into the hands of the *Orthodox* <note>original: "Regtzinnigen"; those adhering to the "right" or "straight" teaching of the Nicene Creed.</note>, forty years after the *Arians* had taken them during the reign of Emperor *Constantinus* <note>The text likely refers here to the era beginning with the sons of Constantine the Great, particularly Constantius II, who favored Arianism and oversaw the removal of Nicene bishops.</note>. After a few months had passed, *Theodosius* issued a new command, by which he forbade the *Arians* from holding any *Meetings* in the cities or in the countryside, and from debating their *Doctrines* against the *Orthodox*, or even speaking of them. In the following year, he had a third command proclaimed, more detailed and powerful than the other two, by which he forbade granting any *refuge* to the Heretics to practice their mysteries there, or tolerating their holding of meetings. He declared that he absolutely desired all his subjects to adhere to the faith of the *Holy Council of Nicaea*, and ordered that all churches be returned to the orthodox Bishops. This decree or command was given at *Constantinople* in the year 381, the Empire being very flourishing at that time. *Theodosius* convened, with the consent of Pope *Damasus*, a *General Assembly* of all orthodox Bishops of the *East* in *Constantinople*, which *Damasus*, after having approved it, honored with the title of a *General Council* <note>This is known historically as the First Council of Constantinople (381 AD), which confirmed the Nicene Creed and expanded the section regarding the Holy Spirit.</note>, because it agreed in *Doctrine* and in *Articles of Faith* with the whole

<margin>Western Church.</margin>
At this assembly <note>Referring to the First Council of Constantinople in 381 CE, the second ecumenical council of the Christian Church.</note> there arrived one hundred and fifty orthodox *Bishops*, several of whom had been *Arians* during the reign of *Valens* <note>Valens was a Roman Emperor who reigned from 364–378 CE and was a notable supporter of Arianism, often persecuting those who held to the Nicene Creed.</note>. The Emperor, believing that the followers of the heresy of *Macedonius* <note>The Macedonians, also called "Pneumatomachi" or "Spirit-fighters," accepted the divinity of the Son but denied the divinity of the Holy Spirit.</note> could easily be brought back to the Church, invited them there, and arranged for thirty-six of them to be received. But because they had the boldness to testify that they would not recognize the *Consubstantiality* <note>original: "Medezelfstandigheid"; this is a Dutch rendering of the Greek term *homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance," used to describe the relationship between the members of the Trinity.</note> of the *Word* <note>original: "Woord"; referring to Jesus Christ as the *Logos*.</note>, they were expelled from the *Council* and stripped of their Bishoprics. The heresy of *Macedonius*, which denied the *Deity* of the *Holy Spirit*, was unanimously condemned, and the *Nicene Creed* <note>original: "Symbolum van Nicea"; the foundational statement of Christian belief established in 325 CE.</note> was confirmed, condemning all the *Sects* of *Arianism*, which, despite the diversity of their doctrines, all agreed in denying *Consubstantiality*. And because this *Creed* says nothing of the *Holy Spirit*, whose *Deity* had not yet been attacked <note>In 325 CE, the primary focus was the relationship between the Father and the Son; the status of the Spirit became a major controversy later in the 4th century.</note>, there was added, by way of explanation, that which had been determined regarding this *adorable Person*. In the year 382, Pope *Damasus* worked with the Emperors to hold a *General Council* in *Rome*. The *Western Bishops* immediately traveled there; but of the *Easterners*, none appeared except *Paulinus*, Patriarch of *Antioch*, *St. Epiphanius*, and *Acholius of Thessalonica*.
