It is claimed that such was in use in the Church since the times of the Apostles: but the *Arians* had corrupted it, saying, "Glory be to the Father, through the Son, in the Holy Spirit," in order not to express the equality of the Divine Persons <note>The Arian version suggested a hierarchy—that the Son and Spirit were subordinate tools or vessels of the Father, rather than equal in essence.</note>. It is also probable that, out of consideration for him, he tolerated the **Arians** and even gave them great freedom in **Constantinople**.

However, one should be somewhat cautious, especially in the beginning, when the *Philosophy* of *Plato* is discussed; for it has not yet been proven that the *Jews* in the time of *Christ* held the views of *Plato*, as the particular views and expressions of *Philo* <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note> cannot be charged to the general account of all his fellow believers. 

And regarding the *Platonic* expressions and idioms which are claimed to be contained in the *New Testament*, such as in *John* 1 and elsewhere—about which Mr. *Le Clercq* <note>Jean Le Clercq (1657–1736), a prominent Genevan theologian and biblical scholar.</note> has provided more detail in his explanation of the prologue to the *Gospel of John*—he has faced opposition in that matter from Messrs. *van der Waeyen* and *Benveſt*, who asserted that those idioms were not taken from *Plato*, but from the *Old Testament*. Mr. *Le Clercq* himself even acknowledges this to have been the view of some *Church Fathers*, who therefore judged that *Plato* had knowledge of the teachings of *Moses* and the *Prophets*. 

Furthermore, when presenting viewpoints, one cannot always rely on the accounts of adversaries; one must be cautious on both sides, and therefore not always give credence to the accounts of *Philostorgius* <note>An Arian church historian of the 4th and 5th centuries whose work survives only in fragments.</note> regarding this matter of the *Arians*, as he was a follower of them, if not entirely, at least in part. For the rest, one can translate the word <term>ὁμοούσιος</term> <note>original: "homoousios"; the orthodox theological term meaning "of the same substance" or "consubstantial," used to describe the relationship between God the Father and God the Son.</note> better as *Self-existent* than as *Co-substantial*, and <term>ὁμοιούσιος</term> <note>original: "homoiousios"; the term used by semi-Arians meaning "of a similar substance."</note> as *Like-existent* than as *Similar-formed*. Two English theologians, *Gardiner* and *Bull* <note>George Bull (1634–1710), whose "Defensio Fidei Nicaenae" defended the orthodoxy of the ante-Nicene Fathers</note>, and a Frenchman *Le Moine*, a Professor at Leiden, have written the *Vindication of the Ancient Fathers* against him.

## ARMENIAN CHRISTIANS,
so named after the province which they inhabit, and where the Apostle *Bartholomew* preached the Gospel, the number of believers growing strongly thereafter. At the beginning of the 4th century, the Church of *Armenia* was very flourishing under Bishop *Gregory* <note>Saint Gregory the Illuminator (c. 257 – c. 331), the patron saint and first official head of the Armenian Apostolic Church</note>, and had the benefit of seeing that not only the *Clergy*, but even the *Laity*, and the *Virgins* <note>Referring to consecrated virgins or nuns</note>, shed their blood...

In the end of the 4th century, it <note>The Armenian Church</note> underwent the second persecution, instigated by the *Arians*: and in the following centuries it stood equally against the Heretics. In those times the *Armenians* were under the ecclesiastical jurisdiction of the *Patriarch of Constantinople*, as they belonged to the Province of the Diocese of *Pontus*: but they separated from him before the time of *Photius* <note>Photius (c. 810–893) was a powerful Patriarch of Constantinople whose tenure was marked by conflict with the Papacy</note>, just like the *Greek Church*, which they have always hated and abhorred. Meanwhile, the *Christian Religion* has maintained itself among them, although not in its original luster and purity. They have two *Patriarchs* <term>Patriarch|A high-ranking bishop in Eastern Christian traditions</term>, one for *Greater*, and the other for *Lesser Armenia*. The first formerly had his Seat at *Sebaste*, and nowadays he resides in a Monastery close to *Erivan* <note>Modern-day Yerevan, the capital of Armenia</note>. The second, whose seat was formerly in *Melitene*, currently resides in the City of *Cis*, near *Tarsus* in *Cilicia* <note>This refers to the Armenian Kingdom of Cilicia, located in what is now southern Turkey</note>. Various changes have crept into the faith of the *Armenians*, which will not all be explained here. They believe that the *Holy Spirit* proceeds from the *Father* alone <note>This refers to the rejection of the "Filioque" clause, a major theological divide regarding whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son, or just the Father</note>, and they also give the *Holy Communion* <note>original: "H. Nagtmaal"</note> to small children; some *Armenians* deprive the *Sacraments* of the power to bestow grace, and though they deny that there is a *Purgatory*, they nonetheless pray for the *Deceased*, imagining that they will not behold the *Heavenly* glory until after the general *resurrection* of the flesh. Besides this, their faith is uniform with that of the *Latin Church*, and they also have a particular *Devotion*

### ARMINIANS, or REMONSTRANTS
is the name of a well-known denomination that arose in the 17th century within the *Reformed Dutch Church*. They are called by the first-mentioned name after their founder and establisher *Jacobus Arminius* <note>Jacobus Arminius (1560–1609) was a Dutch theologian whose views on free will and predestination challenged the strict Calvinism of the time.</note>, and by the latter after a *Remonstrance* <term>Remonstrance|A document of grievances or points of belief; specifically the 1610 petition outlining the five points of Arminianism</term>, delivered by them after the death of *Arminius* to the Lords *States of Holland and West Friesland*, in which they had set forth their doctrine and sentiments.

But when *Arminius* was afterwards called to the professorship in *Leiden*, some strongly opposed it, until *Uitenhogaart* <note>Johannes Wtenbogaert (1557–1644), an influential court preacher and a leader of the Remonstrant movement.</note> finally gained the upper hand and pushed the matter through. Since then, the differences between *Arminius* and *Franciscus Gomarus*, his colleague in the professorship at *Leiden*, increased significantly; those who sided with *Arminius* were called *Arminians*, and those who continued to adhere to the orthodox Church with *Gomarus* were called *Gomarists* <term>Gomarists|The strict Calvinist faction named after Franciscus Gomarus, who opposed the Arminians' views on free will.</term>. answered with a *Defense* or *Apology* <note>In this context, "Apology" refers to a formal written defense of one's opinions or conduct, from the Greek 'apologia'.</note>. *Arminius* finally came to die in the year 1609, but his doctrine did not die with him; for in June of the year 1610, his *Followers* presented a *Remonstrance* <term>Remonstrance|A formal document of protest or dissent. From this document, the Arminians gained the name 'Remonstrants'.</term> to the *Lords States of Holland and West Friesland*, in which they requested that the *Confession of Faith* of the *Netherlands Church* and the *Heidelberg Catechism* <term>Heidelberg Catechism|A Protestant educational document used to teach Reformed Christian doctrine, which the Remonstrants wanted to revise.</term> might be examined; that adherence to the *Five Articles* <note>These five points of doctrine—covering predestination, atonement, grace, and perseverance—became the core of the Arminian-Calvinist debate.</note>, over which the dispute had hitherto existed, might be granted to them, and that they furthermore might be tolerated and admitted as brothers under the protection of the *High Authority*, until a *Free Synod* <term>Synod|A formal council of church leaders convened to decide on matters of doctrine or administration.</term> was held, and all the rest settled therein.

The *Deputies of the Church of Holland*, on the other hand, presented a *Counter-Remonstrance* to the *Lords States*, signed with their names—which the *Arminians* had not done: and because of the aforementioned *Remonstrance*, all the *Arminians* have the name of *Remonstrants*, and because of the last-mentioned *Counter-Remonstrance*, the name of *Counter-Remonstrants* was given to all those who remained with the old Reformed Doctrine, just as the same had been publicly taught in the *Netherlands Churches* from the beginning of the *Reformation*, and with which the Reformed *Churches of other lands* agreed. 

The *Lords States* sought to remove this schism in the following year, 1611, in a *Colloquy* <note>Original Dutch: "Zamenfpraak", referring to a formal dialogue or debate.</note> about the *Five Articles*, held in the *Hague*, and known by the name of the *Hague Conference*, yet the same ended not only fruitlessly, but since then, hearts and minds became even more [embittered] against one another.

On the part of the *Arminians*, *Uitenbogaart* <note>Johannes Wtenbogaert (1557–1644) was a prominent Dutch Remonstrant leader and court preacher at The Hague.</note>, in order to win over the *High Authority* <term>High Authority|The 'Hooge Overigheid' refers to the States of Holland, the sovereign civil governing body of the province.</term>, published a book entitled *The Office of the Authority in Ecclesiastical Matters*, in which he attributes to them the highest judgment and the decision-making power in all *Church Matters*, as well as the appointment of Ministers. This book was refuted, among others, by *Ruardus Acronius*, a Minister at *Schiedam*, using *Uitenbogaart's* own writings.

In the year 1614, the Lords States, in order to prevent any further *estrangement* as much as possible for the time being, issued a *Formula of Unity* under the title *Resolution of the Lords States of Holland and West Friesland for the Peace of the Churches*. In this document, the *Ministers* were prescribed how high and how far they might go in the handling of the *five Articles* <note>These five points regarding salvation and grace were the core theological disagreements between the Remonstrants and their opponents.</note>, and they were further urged toward peace and gentleness. Attached to that *Resolution* was a *Treatise*, full of *citations* from the *Holy Scripture*, the *Councils*, and the *Church Fathers*. However, this was written against by *Jacob Trigland*, *Johan Bogerman*, *Sibrand Lubbert*, and others, who wished to demonstrate that the *High Authority* has no power to make any *Resolution* in such *Church Matters*. *Hugo de Groot* <note>Better known internationally as Hugo Grotius, the famous jurist and philosopher who supported the Remonstrant cause.</note> undertook to refute this in his book named *The Piety of the States of Holland* <note>original: "Pietas Ordinum Hollandiae"</note>, to which he was answered by *Sibrand Lubbert* under the title *Sibrand Lubbert’s Response to the Piety of Hugo Grotius* <note>original: "Sibrandi Lubberti Responsio in pietatem Hugonis Grotii"</note>.

disputes that have come out on both sides, according to the course of the years in a certain work, which he calls *The Peacemaker of the Divided Netherlands* <note>original Latin: *Pacificatorium dissecti Belgii*; at this time, "Belgii" referred generally to the Low Countries or the Netherlands</note>. However, there is reason to doubt whether that list is perfect, as it is very difficult not to skip any from such a multitude of writings that have seen the light regarding this matter.

Their Doctrine, for which they separated themselves from the Reformed, primarily consists of this: *that God has neither chosen nor rejected any person, except in so far as He has foreseen through His omniscience that the person would persevere in Faith, or in unbelief, and in impenitence.* <note>This is the core of Arminianism: that God's election is based on foreseen faith rather than an unconditional decree.</note> They dare to practice their Religion everywhere in *Holland*, except in *Dordrecht*. <note>Dordrecht remained a stronghold of strict Calvinism following the Synod held there in 1618-1619.</note>

### ARTOTYRITES
are followers of a certain sect, sprung from the *Sect* of *Montanus* in the second century. They held the Holy Supper with Bread and Cheese <note>The name comes from the Greek words *artos* (bread) and *tyros* (cheese).</note>, corrupted the Holy Scripture, and granted the Priesthood to Women. *Epiphanius, Heresy 49. Augustine, Heresy 27. Baronius, Year of Christ 373.*

were certain Heretics who arose around the year 173. They held somewhat with the *Montanists*. Every one of them carried a Leather bag with them, to show that they were those whom the Gospel calls "New wineskins filled with Wine." <note>A reference to Matthew 9:17.</note> From this they also had the name of *Ascites*, for *’Askós* <note>Greek: *askos*</note> signifies a Bag or Wineskin among the Greeks. ATHEISTS are those who do not posit a God, or at least have no Religion. *Atheists* are also those called who *lead a Godless life*, and do not concern themselves with Piety or honesty.

---

## B.

BAANITES were Heretics who had their origin from a certain *Baanes*, who presented himself as a *Disciple* of *Epaphroditus*, and spread the errors of the *Manichaeans* <note>The Manichaeans followed a dualistic religion teaching a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness.</note> in the 9th century. <note>original Latin: *Petr. Siculus in Historia Manich. renasc. Baron. an. C. 810.* — Peter Siculus in History of the reborn Manichaeans; Baronius, Year of Christ 810.</note>

BARDESANISTS were Heretics in the East, named thus after *Bardesanes*, who lived in the eleventh <note>The text states the 11th century ("elfde eeuw"), though historically Bardesanes lived in the 2nd and early 3rd centuries.</note> century in *Mesopotamia*, and disputed against the *Valentinians* and *Marcionites*, but thereafter mostly joined their party. He especially denied the *Resurrection of the dead*, and his *Followers* (among whom his *Son Harmonius* gained a great name) adopted even more errors. <note>original Latin: *Euseb. lib. 4. Hist. Eccl. c. 30. August. de Haer. cap. 35. Epiphan. Haer. 56. Strunzii Historia Bardesanis, & Bardesanitarum.* — Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History, book 4, chapter 30; Augustine, On Heresies, chapter 35; Epiphanius, Heresy 56; Strunz’s History of Bardesanes and the Bardesanists.</note>

**BASILIDIANS**, named as such after the Heretic *Basilides*, who was a Disciple of *Simon the Sorcerer* <note>Also known as Simon Magus, a figure from the New Testament often cited by early church writers as the first heretic and the father of Gnosticism.</note>; he lived in the second century and expanded his teachings in *Egypt*. He denied the *Trinity*, and taught much concerning the various origins of the *Angels*, by whom a total of 365 *Heavens* were created, which he encompassed under the name of *Abraxas* <note>A mystical word found in Gnostic texts; in Greek gematria, the letters of Abraxas add up to 365, representing the days of the year or the levels of heaven.</note>, understanding thereby the eternal God. To the lowest sort of his *Angels* he attributed the creation of the earth and of mankind. Among these, he called the most prominent one the *God of the Jews*. He said that God had sent *Christ*, as his only-begotten Son, into the world to deliver mankind from the *Dominion of the Angels*, but this *Christ* had not suffered; rather, it was *Simon of Cyrene* <note>The man who helped carry the cross of Jesus; Basilides claimed Simon was transformed to look like Jesus and died in his place.</note>. Because of this, *Basilides* disparaged the death of the *Martyrs*, saying that in times of persecution one might freely renounce the faith. He maintained that *Souls* had sinned in another life and were punished for it in this one, because God forgave no sins except those committed through *ignorance*. Furthermore, he denied the *resurrection of the flesh*, and for that reason permitted his Followers all fleshly desires. He and his disciples used all manner of strange words, figures, and *Characters* <note>Mystical symbols or sigils used in ritual magic.</note>, with which they practiced their *Sorcery*. He also wrote a distinct *Gos-*

**BEGHARDS**, and **BEGUINES**, were Heretics who took their origin in the *Netherlands* at the end of the 13th century. They lived unmarried, and taught that man can reach ultimate perfection without sinning in this life; that one then had no need to fast or pray; and because the outward feeling was entirely subject to the Spirit, one could allow the body whatever one wanted, without such a person needing to be subject to any human Ordinances. They had a large following in *Germany*, but were first condemned in the year 1166 at the *Council in Vienna* <note>The date 1166 in the source appears to be an error, as it precedes the 13th-century origin mentioned; this may refer to a local synod or a misprint for a later council.</note>, afterwards in the year 1260 in *Cologne*, and finally at the general Council of *Vienne*, in *France* in the year 1311.

The **BEGUINES** consisted of *Women*, and were divided into two kinds. The first took no monastic vows, following the errors of the Beghards, and of *Margaret Porete* <note>A French mystic and author of "The Mirror of Simple Souls," executed for heresy in 1310.</note>. The others were not condemned, living according to the institutions prescribed for them by *St. Begga*, Sister of *St. Gertrude*, or as others maintain, by a certain Priest *Lambert le Bègue* <note>The nickname "le Bègue" means "the Stammerer."</note>. Pope *John* XXII made a distinction between these two kinds; thus today the remains of the

### **BOGOMILS**,
so certain heretics were named in the 12th century, who are said to have denied the **Holy Trinity** and attributed a *human* nature to God. They taught that the **World** was created by the evil angels; they also boasted of having the *Baptism* of Christ, while the rest of Christendom only had the Baptism of *John*. The *Lord's Prayer* they called the *Holy Supper*; they also believed in no other Resurrection than that which occurred through repentance and a Christian life, etc. They also rejected the *veneration* of the Saints and the sacrifice of the *Mass*. However, writers do not agree with one another regarding these Bogomils. It is also held that *Bogomili* in the Slavonic language <note>Specifically Old Church Slavonic; "Bogu milui" roughly translates to "God have mercy."</note> means someone who calls upon the mercy of God; for they were considered very holy people. *Johann Christoph Wolf* published the *History of these Bogomils in Quarto* in the year 1712. *Zygabenus, <note>original: "Zygabenus in dogmat. Panoplia"</note> in the Full Armor of Doctrine, part 2, title 23; Anna Comnena, <note>original: "Anna Comnena de gestis Alexii Commeni"</note> On the Deeds of Alexios Komnenos, book 15; Baronius for the year of Christ 1118; Prateolus; Sanderus; Kortholt, *Ecclesiastical History, 12th Century, Chapter 2.*

<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>BOOK of Heretics.</header>
<sig>M 2</sig>

mixed with Adamites and *Waldensians*. They were actually *Hussites*, refusing to accept the decrees of the *Council of Basel* <note>A 15th-century church council that attempted to address the Hussite wars and ecclesiastical reforms.</note>. After they lost the battle against the *Bohemian Nobility* in the year 1434, and their General *Procopius* <note>Prokop the Great, a prominent Hussite leader who died at the Battle of Lipany.</note> was killed, they held their meetings either in the wilderness, or in *caves*, or in *private houses*, and were therefore called *Speluncales* <note>Latin: "cave dwellers"</note> and *Jamnici* <note>from the Slavic "jamníci," meaning "pit-dwellers"</note>. Finally, King *George Podiebrad* <note>George of Poděbrady, the first non-Catholic king of Bohemia.</note> gave them the Land of *Lititz*, located on the borders of *Silesia* and *Moravia*, where they performed their worship primarily in the village of *Kunwald*, calling themselves *Brothers* and *Sisters* among one another. Later, many prominent families from *Bohemia* and *Moravia* joined them, at which time, under King *Vladislaus* the fourth, they again received various *Churches* and expanded into the neighboring lands.

When *Luther* arose, they sent two *Emissaries* to him, testifying that they were at one with him regarding the matter of doctrine, and had some of his writings translated into the *Bohemian language*. In the year 1535, they submitted their *Confessions of Faith* to the Emperor, which came to light in the year 1538 with a preface by *Luther*. Because they refused to send aid to King *Ferdinand* for the *Schmalkaldic War* <note>A 16th-century war between the Holy Roman Empire and a league of Lutheran princes.</note> after this, their churches were closed in the year 1547 and their *Preachers* were driven out. Thus, many of these Brothers departed for *Poland*, *Prussia*, *Transylvania* <note>original: "Zevenbergen"</note>, and *Switzerland*, partially embracing the views of the *Lutherans* and partially those of the *Reformed*. ...some were still found in *Moravia*, who according to the account of *Theobaldus* <note>Zacharias Theobald (1584–1627), a German historian who wrote a famous account of the Hussite Wars.</note> behaved strangely at their *assembly*, and were called *Kortereller* <note>A derogatory nickname used by the common people, likely a corruption of a foreign term or referring to their distinct appearance or habits.</note> by the common man. They formerly had four *Bishops*, one in *Greater Poland*, one in *Bohemia*, and two in *Moravia*, sharing equal authority; just as they also decided in the year 1500 that one should not be superior to the others. Furthermore, they had *Co-bishops* <note>original: "Coëpiscopi"</note> or *Co-elders* <note>original: "Conseniones"</note>, who fulfilled the role of the *Bishops* in their absence, also sat in the *Consistory* <note>The governing council of the church.</note>, and must have reached at least 35 years of age.

Additionally, among them were:
1. *Preachers*, being 30 years of age and having a good testimony from the congregation in which they had lived. 
2. *Deacons*, who were ordained and allowed to administer Baptism and the [Lord's] Supper; but they also had to earn a living through their manual trade <note>A practice intended to ensure ministers were not a financial burden and remained humble.</note> to support both themselves and other ministers of the Church. 
3. *Acolytes*, who were instructed in the church service by the *Bishops* or *Elders*. The eldest among these were required to lead *catechetical exercises* <note>Teaching through questions and answers.</note> and sometimes *Preach*. 

Within the congregation itself were: 
1. *Learners*, or *Novices*, who were either born among them or had come over from the *Roman Church*, and allowed themselves to be instructed in matters of *Religion*. 
2. The *Instructed*, who were admitted to the *Holy Supper*, having learned to give an *Account* <note>A formal statement or defense of their personal beliefs.</note> of their faith. 
3. Those *striving for perfection*, applying themselves above all to renouncing the world and to the...

**BOEHMISTS** <note>Dutch: Böhmisten</note> are the followers of the famous *Jacob Boehme*, who was born in the year 1575 in *Alt-Seidenburg*, a village near *Görlitz* in *Upper Lusatia* <note>original: "Opper-Launits," a region in eastern Germany.</note>, born to humble peasants; he was a *shoemaker* in *Görlitz* and died there in the year 1624. His writings are very difficult to understand. See *Frankenberg's life of Jacob Boehme. Arnold's History of Heretics, book 17, chapter 19, p. 2.*

### BORELISTS

, a certain type of Christians in Holland, so named after *Adam Boreel*, a Zeelander <note>A native of the Dutch province of Zeeland.</note> by birth, and infected with Socinianism <note>Socinianism was a rationalist Christian movement that denied the Trinity and the divinity of Christ.</note>, but otherwise a learned man, and very experienced in the Hebrew language. He wrote a work under the title of *To the Law and the Testimony* <note>original: "ad legem & testimonium"</note>, against which *Hoornbeek*, *Maresius*, and others have written. Furthermore, there is from him in the Dutch language, *Golden Christian Concatenation* <note>original: "Concatenatio aurea Christiana"</note>, and *Treatise on the Fraternal Religion* <note>original: "Tractatus de fraterna Religione"</note>. He died in the year 1666; his followers were called Borelists in Holland, of whom Mr. *Stoup* gives an account in his treatise on the *Religion of the Dutch*. Their way of life is very strict, and they spend most of their goods on alms; they care neither for sacraments, public prayers, nor any other public worship. When reading the *Holy Scripture*, they also will not tolerate any human interpretations. *Sandius* in *Library of the Anti-Trinitarians* <note>original: "Bibl. Anti-Trinit."</note>. *Arnold's History of Heretics*, volume 2, book 17, chapter 13, etc.

<meta>catchword: BROW-</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the description of the Borborites' alleged rituals and introduces the Borelists, a 17th-century Dutch ascetic group founded by Adam Boreel that rejected institutional church structures and interpretation of Scripture.</summary>

BR of Heretics.
M 4
<meta>catchword: vee-</meta>

## BROWNISTS

This is the name given to a certain religious group that emerged in England during the 16th century, and who are also counted among the Independents. They are said to have their origin with *Robert Bolton*, who was the very first in England to separate himself over the matter of Church governance. After him, *Robert Brown* joined them; he was first a preacher in *London*, and it is from him that they received their name. He traveled from *England* to *Middelburg* in *Zeeland*, but later returned and recanted his opinions, being received back into the *English Church* <note>The Church of England or Anglican Church.</note>. Following this, *Henry Barrow* joined the group, though he, along with his associate *Greenwood*, was put to death in the year 1593 by order of Queen *Elizabeth*. The *Brownists* called them *Martyrs*, and relocated from *England* to *Amsterdam*, where they had two leaders, *Francis Johnson* and *Henry Ainsworth*, who published their confession of faith in the year 1602. After these events, some stayed in *Emden*, *Leiden*, and elsewhere, while some remained in *England*. *John Robinson* was held in high esteem among them, writing an *Apology* <note>original: "Apologie"; a formal written defense of one's religious position.</note> for himself and his followers. Regarding their way of life, they themselves confess that they agree with the *Reformed Church* on the primary *Articles of Faith*, but they reject the *English Church governance*, the *heathen names* <note>The Brownists objected to using names for days of the week (like Thursday/Thor or Friday/Frigg) that were derived from pagan gods.</note> of the days, and the reading of *heathen books* <note>This refers to the use of classical Greek and Roman literature in education, which some radical reformers viewed as worldly or corrupting.</note>. Others attribute further errors to them, namely, that they reject both *Episcopal* <note>Governance by bishops.</note> and *Presbyterian* <note>Governance by elders or presbyters.</note> church government, at least in the latter

<summary>This page describes the origins and key figures of the Brownists, an English Separatist movement that fled to the Netherlands to escape persecution and establish their own form of church governance.</summary>
<keywords>Brownists, Robert Brown, Henry Barrow, Independents, Amsterdam, Church Governance, religious exile, 16th century, Separatists</keywords>

## PETROBRUSIANS
<note>original Dutch: "Brusianen"</note>, so named after *Peter of
Bruys* or *Brusius*, who lived in the year 1120.
He was a Canon at *Lucca* in *Italy*, although he
voluntarily laid down his Order. He sided
with Emperor *Henry IV* against Pope *Gre-
gory VII*. But he was later, as a
*Heretic*, accused of many errors, name-
ly that he had rejected *Infant Baptism*, *Churches* <note>Referring here to the physical buildings rather than the spiritual community.</note>,
*the worship and veneration of Crosses*, the *Offer-
ing of the Mass*, and *Prayers for the Deceased*. 
His teaching was spread by
him everywhere, especially in *Provence*, *Lan-
guedoc*, and *Gascony*, being
greatly supported by his chief fol-
lower *Henry*, after whom the *Henricians* are 
named. But he was condemned as a *Heretic* 
in the *Council of Toulouse* in the year 1119, 
and at the Second *Coun-
cil of Lateran* in the year 1126,
and finally in the year 1147, at
*Saint-Gilles on the Rhône* <note>original Dutch: "St. Gillis aan de Rhone"</note> burned alive. *Ar-
nold's History of Heretics* book 12, chapter 5, § 11 and 12.
*Baronius*, etc.

CA of the Heretics.
M 5
<meta>catchword: century</meta>

### C.

**CAINITES**, **CAINIANS**, were certain Heretics, a type of *Gnostics*, in the 2nd century after the birth of Christ. They derived their name from *Cain*, whom they claimed was formed by a *heavenly* and *Almighty power*, whereas *Abel*, on the contrary, was said to have been made by a much weaker power. They believed that the way to salvation consisted in seeking to experience all kinds of things and seeking to follow one’s desires in all manner of wicked undertakings. They held various ridiculous opinions regarding a great number of Angels, to whom they gave *Barbarian Names* <note>This refers to non-Greek, often Hebrew-sounding magical names used in Gnostic rituals</note>, and to each of whom they attributed a specific sin. Thus, if they were caught in a bad deed, they pleaded for help from that Angel who, in their opinion, was set over it. They forged a godless book under the title of the *Ascension of St. Paul*, which they presented as the secret words that the Apostle had heard during his ecstasy <note>Referring to the biblical account of Paul being "caught up to the third heaven"</note>. They also showed a singular respect for *Cain*, *Korah* <note>original: "Coran"</note>, *Dathan*, *Abiram* <note>Biblical figures who famously rebelled against the authority of Moses</note>, the *Sodomites*, and especially for *Judas*, because his betrayal had caused the death of Christ: They also made use of a certain *Gospel* bearing the name of this false Apostle. *Tertullian, On the Prescription of Heretics* <note>original Latin: "de præsc."</note> *chapters 33, 47, etc. Jerome* <note>original Latin: "Hironym."</note> *book 1, chapter 34. Epiphanius, Against Heresies* <note>original Latin: "hæreſ."</note> *38. Augustine, chapter 18, On Heresies* <note>original Latin: "de her."</note>. *Baronius, Year of Christ 145* <note>original Latin: "a. C. 145"</note>.

---

# Alphabetical List of Names CA

136

century, named the *Hussites* in *Bohemia*, because they carried a *Chalice*—called *Calix* in Latin—in their *banners*, and specifically desired to restore the use of the *Chalice* in the *Holy Supper* <note>The Eucharist or Communion</note>. *Master Jacobellus* <note>Jacob of Mies (1372–1429), a colleague of Jan Hus</note> brought this doctrine back to light at the time when *John Hus* had appeared at the *Council of Constance* <note>original Dutch: "Kerkvergadering van Costnits"; a 15th-century ecumenical council that condemned Hus</note>. A dispute had arisen as to whether *John Hus* approved of that opinion. They wrote to him in *Constance* regarding this, and he answered from his prison that he fully approved of that teaching; therefore, those who persevered in it were called **CALIXTINES**. 

However, one must note that this name was mostly given to those who did not exactly agree with the *Hussites* on the other *Articles of Faith* <note>original: "Geloofsstukken"</note>, and primarily sought the use of the *Chalice* in the *Holy Supper*. Although the use of the *Chalice* was strictly forbidden in the 12th session of the *Council of Constance*, the *Calixtines* were nonetheless even more incited by this to maintain their teaching. But when in the *Council of Basel* <note>original: "Consilie van Basel"</note> in the year 1431, most were persuaded to reach an agreement—since they were granted the *Supper* under both forms <note>The "Utraquist" practice of receiving both bread and wine</note>, along with other points—those who reunited with the *Roman Church* in this way were specifically named *Calixtines*. 

On the contrary, the others, who remained steadfastly with *John Hus* and his teaching, were named *Taborites*, *Orebites*, or *Orphans* <note>original: "Orphani"</note>; likewise *Picardians* and *Boleslavians*, and they opposed the *Calixtines*, so that the...
